in EBflow and Langevin-MCEM relative to

1/\lambda_{\max}
, i.e. all further reference to
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}
will be
in the following reparametrized form of (
2.20
):

{\bm{\varphi}}_{t+1}={\bm{\varphi}}_{t}-\frac{\eta^{\varphi}_{t}}{\lambda_{\max}}\Bigg[\mathbf{X}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\mathbf{X}{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}-\mathbf{y})+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\bigg(\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{t,k}(\varphi_{t,j}-b_{k})\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi-b_{k})}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{t,k}\,\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi_{t,j}-b_{k})}\bigg)_{j=1}^{p}\Bigg]+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta^{\varphi}_{t}}{\lambda_{\max}}}\,\mathbf{b}_{t}.

(4.3)

Step sizes.
A larger step size

\eta_{t}^{\varphi}
in EBflow leads to faster mixing, but can result in a higher
discretization bias for ULA. The step size
\eta_{t}^{w}
determines an exponential
weighting of past iterates when updating the prior. An overly large

\eta_{t}^{w}
can lead to high Monte Carlo variance in the estimated prior,
while an overly small
\eta^{w}_{t}
can lead to slow convergence.

To illustrate these trade-offs, we generate data
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}
with
dimensions
n=500
and
p=1000
, with standard Gaussian
prior
g_{\ast}=\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, i.i.d. Gaussian design
\mathbf{X}
, and
noise variance
\sigma^{2}
chosen
7
7

7

In our experiment, we first generate

\mathbf{X}
and
{\bm{\theta}}
, and then set
\sigma^{2}
equal to the sample variance of the entries of
\mathbf{X}\theta
.
so that
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
and
\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}
each explain 50%
of the variance of
\mathbf{y}
.
Let
w_{\ast,1},\ldots,w_{\ast,K}
denote weights representing
g_{*}
discretized
to the grid
b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}\in[-3,3]
.
Figure
1
(a) displays the total-variation (TV) error
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(g_{t},g_{\ast})\approx\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}|w_{t,k}-w_{\ast,k}|

of the density estimate for 10,000 iterations of EBflow, over 10 independent
trials on the same data
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{y})
. To produce a smooth density estimate,
we apply all methods to minimize the negative log-likelihood objective
with an additional smoothing spline regularizer as in
(
2.24
), with common fixed regularization
\lambda=0.003
.

In this example, fixing larger step sizes

\eta^{\varphi}_{t}=1.0
and
\eta^{w}_{t}=0.01
results in reasonable performance,
with smaller
(\eta_{t}^{w},\eta_{t}^{\varphi})
leading to slower convergence and
larger
\eta_{t}^{w}/\eta_{t}^{\varphi}
leading to higher Monte Carlo variance in the
estimated prior. There is a small amount of ULA bias incurred by the large step
size choice
\eta^{\varphi}_{t}=1.0
, and
Figure
1
(b) displays the TV error under an
alternative log-linear step size decay over 10,000 iterations,

\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=a\cdot b^{t}\text{ with }\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=1.0\text{ at }t=1\text{ and }\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=0.1\text{ at }t=10000,\qquad\eta_{t}^{w}=0.01\eta_{t}^{\varphi}.

(4.4)

The reduction of ULA bias at the final step size
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=0.1

yields a reduction of the average TV error of the estimated density
to 0.046, from 0.075 for the fixed choice
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=1.0
. We recommend using this log-linear decay
(
4.4
) as the default option for problems of this size, with
the possibility of increasing
\eta_{t}^{w}
relative to
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}
for
problems with higher dimension
p
.

Analogous trade-offs apply for the other MCEM approaches when determining the
number of iterations
T_{\text{iter}}
between prior updates.
Figure
1
(c–d) displays TV error for Langevin-MCEM with

\eta_{t}^{\varphi}\in\{1.0,0.1\}
and
T_{\text{iter}}\in\{100,1000\}
, and for
Gibbs-MCEM with
T_{\text{iter}}\in\{10,100,1000\}
, both across 10
independent trials for the same data
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{y})
.
We will henceforth fix
T_{\text{iter}}=100
for both
methods to achieve a reasonable
balance between Monte Carlo variance and convergence speed.

4.2
Method comparison

Priors and designs.
We generate data
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}

where
\{\theta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p}\overset{iid}{\sim}g_{\ast}
, and the prior
distribution
g_{\ast}
is one of four smooth densities:

•

(Gaussian)
g_{\ast}=\mathcal{N}(0,1)

•

(Cauchy)
g_{\ast}=\operatorname{Cauchy}(0,0.6)

•

(Skew)
g_{\ast}=\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{N}(-2,0.5^{2})+\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{N}(-1.5,1^{2})+\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{N}(0,2^{2})

•

(Bimodal)
g_{\ast}=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-1.5,0.5^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(1.5,0.5^{2})

For consistency, each prior is truncated to
[-3,3]
and discretized over a grid of
K=61
points

b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}
with equal spacing 0.1. We generate
\mathbf{X}
to be one of the following designs:

•

(identity)
\mathbf{X}=\operatorname{Id}
and
n=p
.

•

(iid)
\mathbf{X}
has i.i.d. Gaussian entries.

•

(block02corr0.9)
\mathbf{X}
has multivariate Gaussian rows, consisting of

p/2
independent pairs of variables with correlation 0.9.

•

(block10corr0.5)
\mathbf{X}
has multivariate Gaussian rows, consisting of

p/10
independent blocks of variables with pairwise correlation 0.5.

We fix the dimension
p=1000
for all designs. For the latter three random
designs, we vary the sample sizes
n\in\{500,1000,2000\}
.
The noise variance
\sigma^{2}
is set
so that
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
and
\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}
each explain 50% of the variance of
\mathbf{y}

for the Gaussian prior, and
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
explains 20% of the variance of
\mathbf{y}

for the Cauchy, skew, and bimodal priors. To study the effects of Monte Carlo variance of the MCEM methods on the estimated prior, we fix a single
generated instance
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{y})
for each prior and design, and then evaluate 10 independent runs of each algorithm
(except for CAVI which is deterministic).

Experimental setup.
We fix the prior support for all methods
to be the grid
b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}
over
[-3,3]
.
EBflow is run with
\tau^{2}
set as in (
4.2
), and the
log-linear step size decay (
4.4
) over 10,000 iterations.
We tested also a preconditioned variant
[
RS02
]

of EBflow with the
{\bm{\varphi}}
update in
(
4.3
) replaced by

{\bm{\varphi}}_{t+1}={\bm{\varphi}}_{t}-\eta^{\varphi}_{t}\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\Bigg[\mathbf{X}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\mathbf{X}{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}-\mathbf{y})+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\bigg(\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{t,k}(\varphi_{t,j}-b_{k})\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi-b_{k})}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{t,k}\,\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi_{t,j}-b_{k})}\bigg)_{j=1}^{p}\Bigg]+\sqrt{2\eta^{\varphi}_{t}}\mathbf{Q}^{-1/2}\,\mathbf{g}_{t},

(4.5)

fixing
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{X}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}\mathbf{X}+\tau^{-2}\operatorname{Id}
as a rough proxy for the
Hessian (
4.1
). Related forms of preconditioning have been
suggested, for example, for posterior sampling in logistic regression models in

[
Dal17
]
to accelerate mixing of Langevin dynamics with
ill-conditioned designs. One may check that this preconditioning corresponds
to the original gradient flow (
1.7
) with an alternative
choice of inner-product on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
underlying the Wasserstein-2 geometry.
Langevin-MCEM and Gibbs-MCEM are run fixing
T_{\text{iter}}=100
Monte Carlo iterations
between M-step updates, also over 10,000 total Monte Carlo iterations.
CAVI is run for 1000 iterations, as it converges much faster than the MCEM methods.

We compare prior density estimates and computation time for all
methods applied to minimize the negative log-likelihood with a small
smoothing spline regularization (
2.24
), fixing

\lambda=0.003
for the Gaussian prior and
\lambda=0.001
for the other
three priors. We report also the achieved marginal log-likelihood for identity
design and mean-squared prediction error for random designs, and verify that
these are similar with and without smoothness regularization.

Figure 2
:
Estimated prior densities across 6 methods, and 10 independent runs
for each method except CAVI. Priors left to right: Gaussian, Cauchy, skew,
bimodal. All settings have sample size
n=1000
and dimension
p=1000
. Design is block02corr0.9.

Results.
Figure
2
displays the
estimated priors in an illustrative example of the block02corr0.9
design with
n=p=1000
, which highlights some of the main trends across other
priors and designs.

In general, the estimates of EBflow, Langevin-MCEM with large step size

\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=1.0
, and Gibbs-MCEM are more accurate than the remaining two
methods. Preconditioning EBflow can yield more stable estimates, due to faster
mixing of the ULA chain.
Langevin-MCEM at the smaller step size
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=0.1
suffers
from high Monte Carlo variance. CAVI produces density
estimates that tend to underestimate the prior variance, due to inaccuracy of
the variational approximation for this type of design that has strong
pairwise correlations between variables.

Table 4
:
Comparison of methods: Bimodal prior.

Tables
1
–
4

summarize estimation accuracy and runtime across
all tested methods, priors, and designs. We report the mean and standard
deviation of the TV error of the estimated density, across 10
independent runs for all methods except CAVI.
As rough proxies for convergence speed, we report
the median number of iterations (rounded to the nearest 100) used to reach a TV
error less than 0.2, or NA if the final TV error is larger than this
value.
8
8

8

The real compute times of Langevin-MCEM and Gibbs-MCEM are
slower than EBflow in our straightforward implementations of these methods.
For Langevin-MCEM, this is due to the complexity of solving the sequence-model NPMLE problem
in each M-step (which is not reflected in the iteration count). For Gibbs
sampling, it is due to a loop over coordinates as compared to a vectorized
matrix-vector computation in EBflow. In our experiments at this problem size, EBflow was about 2–3 times faster than Gibbs sampling, and 5–10 times faster than Langevin-MCEM,
although these comparisons are necessarily implementation dependent.

We report also the attained marginal log-likelihood value
\bar{F}_{n}
(up
to an additive constant) for identity design,
9
9

9

For general design, it is difficult to evaluate the marginal log-likelihood which involves a
p
-dimensional integral.
and the
relative mean-squared prediction error

\text{MSE}=\frac{\|\mathbf{X}_{\text{new}}({\bm{\theta}}_{\ast}-\mathbb{E}[{\bm{\theta}}\mid\mathbf{X},\mathbf{y}])\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{X}_{\text{new}}{\bm{\theta}}_{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}}

across
n_{\text{new}}=1000
test samples
\mathbf{X}_{\text{new}}
for random designs,
where
{\bm{\theta}}_{\ast}
is the true regression parameter. The posterior mean

\mathbb{E}[{\bm{\theta}}\mid\mathbf{X},\mathbf{y}]
is estimated in EBflow and Langevin-MCEM
from
T^{\prime}=50{,}000
additional iterates of
{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}
using
(
2.23
),
T^{\prime}=50{,}000
additional iterates of
{\bm{\theta}}_{t}

in Gibbs-MCEM, and the average of the final posterior marginals estimated in CAVI.

Table
5
compares results with and without spline
smoothing, for a single run of each method at dimensions
n=p=1000
.
The smoothing regularization (
2.24
) is needed to obtain
accurate prior density estimates, but we observe that marginal log-likelihood
and prediction MSE are nearly identical with and without smoothing for all
methods except CAVI (where the prediction MSE without smoothing
is sometimes worse).

:
Comparison of results with (left) and without (right) spline
smoothing

We summarize here a few high-level trends and take-aways from these results,
many of which are also visually apparent in Figure
2
:

•

Mean TV-accuracy of the prior density
is similar for EBflow with and without preconditioning.
However, preconditioning can reduce Monte Carlo
variance across independent trials, especially for
more correlated designs.

•

Mean TV-accuracy of the prior density for EBflow is competitive
with the best tunings of Langevin-MCEM and Gibbs-MCEM for the
Gaussian, Cauchy, and skew priors, and in many settings EBflow yields the
lowest error.

•

For the bimodal prior and many of the tested designs, no method obtains
an accurate prior density estimate with TV error less than 0.2.
In settings where an accurate estimate is obtained, Gibbs-MCEM produces the best
estimates, likely because it more rapidly mixes between the modes of
the posterior distribution in comparison to Langevin dynamics, even when the
latter is carried out in the smoothed parameter
{\bm{\varphi}}
.

•

In terms of iteration count, EBflow has competitive runtime with
Langevin-MCEM and Gibbs-MCEM.

•

CAVI is the fastest method, and is accurate under the
identity design
\mathbf{X}=\operatorname{Id}
(where the variational approximation is exact)
as well as several settings of i.i.d. Gaussian design. However, its
accuracy in terms of both the prior density estimate and prediction MSE
can substantially degrade under correlated designs.

•

All methods achieve similar marginal log-likelihood under
identity design, and the MCEM methods achieve
similar prediction MSE under random designs. We do note
that Langevin-MCEM and EBflow with final step size
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=0.1

have higher per-variable autocorrelation than with step size

\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=1.0
and than Gibbs sampling, resulting in the need to use more posterior
samples to obtain an accurate estimate of
\mathbb{E}[{\bm{\theta}}\mid\mathbf{X},\mathbf{y}]
.

5
Discussion

We have studied empirical Bayes estimation of an unknown prior distribution for
the regression coefficients of a
linear model, introducing a system of bivariate gradient flow
equations for optimizing the marginal log-likelihood via its Gibbs variational
representation. This gradient flow system may be simulated using a Langevin
diffusion, where the prior is continuously updated based on an estimate of the
average marginal distribution of coordinates of the Langevin sample.
Although the regression model and Langevin sample are both high-dimensional, the
one-dimensional nature of the estimand
g_{\ast}
enables its estimation using a
fully nonparametric approach. Similar ideas can be explored in
models beyond linear regression, where a high-dimensional latent
parameter is comprised of independent draws from an unknown
univariate or low-dimensional prior law.

We believe there are many interesting directions for future work, and we discuss
here two of these directions:

1.

(Global landscape analysis) For general regression designs, we believe
the optimization landscape of the marginal log-likelihood
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
may have
multiple local minimizers, and we find empirically that the prior density estimated by EBflow
is sometimes sensitive to the choice of initialization. In such settings, we
have only provided a theoretical guarantee of convergence to a prior
g

that satisfies an approximate first-order optimality condition for
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
.

It is an open question to obtain a better understanding of the global landscape
geometry of
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
, and to characterize possible conditions under which
the local minimizer of
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
may be unique. One interesting setting
is that of random “mean-field” designs, in which
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
may
asymptotically converge to a deterministic limit
functional
\bar{F}(g)
in the sense of Proposition

3.8
. Although simpler algorithms may be
available for empirical Bayes estimation in mean-field contexts, we believe that
nonetheless this is a natural setting to study
questions about the optimization landscape, as they may be studied via the
limit functional
\bar{F}(g)
.

2.

(Time discretization and propagation-of-chaos) In this work we have
studied the dynamics and convergence of the idealized continuous-time gradient
flow, but have not yet taken the steps to translate this to
convergence guarantees for the time-discretized EBflow algorithm.
We believe that a careful analysis of the EBflow algorithm may lead to improved
understanding of issues related to parameter tuning, such as the choice of
variance
\tau^{2}
in our reparametrization by the smoothed regression variable

{\bm{\varphi}}
.

One particular question of interest is to understand conditions under which the
true law
\bar{q}_{t}
may be consistently estimated by the empirical average of
coordinates (
2.18
) over a finite time horizon
[0,T]
, using
only one realization
\{{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
of the Langevin diffusion
rather than an asymptotically growing number of independent parallel chains. We
note that this type of “single-chain propagation-of-chaos” result is only
possible in settings of growing dimensions
p\to\infty
, and we believe that
its study may lead to improved understanding of functional inequalities and
concentration of measure for the law
\{q_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
as it evolves
along the gradient flow.

Our numerical simulations suggest that EBflow is competitive in many settings
with alternative approaches such as MCEM with coordinate-wise Gibbs sampling,
in terms of both estimation accuracy and runtime. More importantly, we hope
that this EBflow approach can make available a broader toolbox of ideas related
to Langevin-based algorithms for empirical Bayes estimation,
including ideas around adaptive preconditioning
[
GC11
]

and underdamped/Hamiltonian dynamics
[
Nea11
,
CCBJ18
]

to handle more ill-conditioned designs, and stochastic gradient
approximations
[
WT11
,
MCF15
]
to scale to
larger-
(n,p)
problems. In extensions to more complex models where
latent parameters are organized in multi-layer archictectures,
such gradient-based ideas rather than coordinate-based computations may become even more advantageous
computationally, due to the lower complexity of computing gradients using a
back-propagation procedure. We believe that such extensions to more complex
models and sampling algorithms would be interesting to explore also in future
work.

Appendix A
Proof of consistency

In this appendix, we prove Theorem
3.1
and Proposition

3.2
.

A.1
TV lower bound

For any
\eta>0
and
g\in\mathcal{P}(M)
, we write as shorthand

g_{\eta}=\mathcal{N}_{\eta}*g=\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2})*g
. Let

\displaystyle d_{\eta}(g,g^{\prime})=W_{1}\big(g_{\eta},g_{\eta}^{\prime}\big)

(A.1)

be the smoothed Wasserstein-1 distance on
\mathcal{P}(M)
.

All constants in the lemmas and proofs may depend on the noise variance

\sigma^{2}>0
, on
M
defining the common support of
\mathcal{P}
,
on the bound
\gamma
for
n/p
, and on

C_{\ast},c_{\ast},\alpha,\beta
in Assumption
A
.

Lemma A.1
.

There exists a sufficiently large constant
v>0
, constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
, and
a sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
such that for all large
n,p
and all

g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(M)
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{g}(\mathbf{y}),P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y}))\geq 1-2e^{-cp(d_{v}(g,g^{\prime})-\delta_{n})_{+}^{2}}.

Proof.

Fixing any constant
\eta>0
, the smoothed distribution
g_{\eta}
satisfies an
LSI with uniformly bounded LSI constant for all

g\in\mathcal{P}(M)

[
BGMZ18
, Theorem 1.1]
. Note that convolving
g

with
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
corresponds to adding an independent
\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2}\,\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top})

noise vector to
\mathbf{y}
. Hence by the data processing inequality,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(P_{g}(\mathbf{y}),P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})\big)\geq d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(P_{g_{\eta}}(\mathbf{y}),P_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})\big),

and it suffices to lower bound the TV distance on the right side for priors

g_{\eta}
and
g_{\eta}^{\prime}
.

Noting that
g_{\eta}
has variance at most
M^{2}+\eta^{2}
for every
g\in\mathcal{P}(M)
, we pick a constant
v>0
satisfying

v^{2}\geq\max_{j\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\eta^{2}+v_{j}\cdot(M^{2}+\eta^{2})+w_{j}\right),\qquad\text{ where }v_{j}=\sum_{k:k\neq j}(\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k})^{2},\qquad w_{j}=\sigma^{2}\|\mathbf{z}_{j}\|_{2}^{2},

(A.2)

and
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{S}}
are given by Assumption
A
. We will
check below that
v_{j},w_{j}
are bounded above by a constant uniformly over

j\in\mathcal{S}
, so that we may indeed pick
v
here as a constant independent of

n,p
. Denote
\Delta=d_{v}(g,g^{\prime})=W_{1}(g_{v},g_{v}^{\prime})
.
To establish the desired TV lower bound, it suffices to exhibit an explicit test statistic
T(\mathbf{y})
with rejection threshold

t=(\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})]+\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})])/2
such that

\displaystyle\max(\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})<t],\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})\geq t])\leq e^{-cp(\Delta-\delta_{n})_{+}^{2}}.

(A.3)

Indeed, this immediately implies the conclusion of the lemma,

d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{g_{\eta}}(\mathbf{y}),P_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y}))\geq\Big|\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})\geq t]-\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})\geq t]\Big|\geq 1-2e^{-cp(\Delta-\delta_{n})_{+}^{2}}.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
and
\kappa_{1}^{\prime},\kappa_{2}^{\prime}
denote the means and
variances of
g_{\eta},g_{\eta}^{\prime}
respectively. To construct
T(\mathbf{y})
satisfying
(
A.3
), we consider three cases. The constants

C,c>0
below will change from instance to instance.

Case 1:

|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{1}^{\prime}|>c_{1}\Delta
for a constant
c_{1}>0
to be
specified. Suppose
without loss of generality
\kappa_{1}>\kappa_{1}^{\prime}
, and consider the test
statistic

\displaystyle T(\mathbf{y})=\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}+\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}.

Under Assumption
A
.2, we have

\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})]-\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})]=\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}\cdot(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{1}^{\prime})\geq c\Delta p.

(A.4)

Computing the gradient of
T(\mathbf{y})
in
({\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
, we observe that

T(\mathbf{y})
is
L
-Lipschitz in
({\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
with respect to
the
\ell_{2}
-norm, where

L^{2}=\mathbf{1}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}\leq Cp

under Assumption
A
.1–2.
Then by the LSI for
({\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
under both
g_{\eta}
and
g_{\eta}^{\prime}
,
for any
s>0
,
T(\mathbf{y})
has the sub-Gaussian concentration arounds its mean

\displaystyle\max\Big(\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})-\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}T(\mathbf{y})\leq-s\Delta p],\;\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})-\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}T(\mathbf{y})\geq s\Delta p]\Big)\leq e^{-cs^{2}\Delta^{2}p}.

Choosing a constant
s>0
small enough and
combining with (
A.4
), this shows that (
A.3
) holds with
\delta_{n}=0
.

Case 2:

|\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{2}^{\prime}|\geq c_{2}\max(\Delta,\Delta^{2})

for a constant
c_{2}>0
to be specified. Suppose without loss of generality

\kappa_{2}>\kappa_{2}^{\prime}
, let
\Pi
denote the projection orthogonal to
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}
,
and consider the test statistic

\displaystyle T(\mathbf{y})=\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{y}\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi(\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{\varepsilon}})\|^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{\theta}}\\
{\bm{\varepsilon}}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}&\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\\
\Pi\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}&\Pi\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\end{pmatrix}}_{:=M(\mathbf{X},\Pi)}\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{\theta}}\\
{\bm{\varepsilon}}\end{pmatrix}

Under Assumptions
A
.1–2, the matrix
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}
has at
least
cp
eigenvalues greater than
c
, for some sufficiently small constant

c>0
. Then, irrespective of the means
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{G_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})]-\mathbb{E}_{G_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})]=\operatorname{Tr}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}\cdot(\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{2}^{\prime})\geq c\max(\Delta,\Delta^{2})p.

We have also
\|M(\mathbf{X},\Pi)\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq C
and
\|M(\mathbf{X},\Pi)\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq Cp
for the above matrix
M(\mathbf{X},\Pi)
, the latter holding because each
block of
M(\mathbf{X},\Pi)
has rank at most
p
.
Then by the Hanson-Wright inequality
[
RV13
, Theorem 1.1]
, viewing
T(\mathbf{y})
as a quadratic form in
({\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
where
{\bm{\theta}}
is sub-Gaussian under both
g_{\eta}
and
g_{\eta}^{\prime}
, for any
s>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})-\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}T(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle\leq-s\max(\Delta,\Delta^{2})p]\leq e^{-c\min(s,s^{2})\Delta^{2}p}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})-\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}T(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle\geq s\max(\Delta,\Delta^{2})p]\leq e^{-c\min(s,s^{2})\Delta^{2}p}.

Then (
A.3
) holds also in this case with
\delta_{n}=0
.

Case 3:

|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{1}^{\prime}|<c_{1}\Delta
and
|\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{2}^{\prime}|<c_{2}\max(\Delta,\Delta^{2})
. Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,p\}
and
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}:j\in\mathcal{S}\}
be as in Assumption
A
. For each
j\in\mathcal{S}
, consider

\displaystyle s_{j}=\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{y}=\underbrace{\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot\theta_{j}}_{\equiv a_{j}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k:k\neq j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k}\cdot\theta_{k}}_{\equiv b_{j}}+\underbrace{\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}}_{\equiv e_{j}},

where these three terms
a_{j},b_{j},e_{j}
are independent. Let

\mathcal{L}(f({\bm{\theta}}))
denote the law of
f({\bm{\theta}})
under
g_{\eta}
. Then
by Assumption
A
.3,

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mathcal{L}(a_{j}),g_{\eta})=W_{1}(\mathcal{L}(a_{j}),\mathcal{L}(\theta_{j}))\leq|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}-1|\cdot\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}[|\theta_{j}|]\to 0.

For
b_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}[b_{j}]=m_{j}\kappa_{1},\qquad\operatorname{Var}_{g_{\eta}}[b_{j}]=v_{j}\kappa_{2},\qquad\text{where }m_{j}=\sum_{k:k\neq j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k},\qquad v_{j}=\sum_{k:k\neq j}(\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k})^{2}.

The Wasserstein-
(2+\beta)
CLT of
[
Rio09
, Eq. (4.1)]
shows that for some
c=c(\beta)>0
,

\displaystyle W_{2+\beta}\Big(\mathcal{L}(b_{j}),\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2})\Big)^{2+\beta}\leq c\sum_{k:k\neq j}|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k}|^{2+\beta}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}[|\theta_{k}|^{2+\beta}].

Assumption
A
.3 provides the Lyapunov condition ensuring that the
right side converges to 0 as
n,p\to\infty
, uniformly over
j\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then also

\displaystyle W_{1}\Big(\mathcal{L}(b_{j}),\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2})\Big)\leq W_{2+\beta}\Big(\mathcal{L}(b_{j}),\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2})\Big)\to 0.

Finally,
e_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,w_{j})
with variance
w_{j}=\sigma^{2}\|\mathbf{z}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
. Putting this together,

W_{1}\Big(\mathcal{L}(s_{j}),\,g_{\eta}*\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j})\Big)\leq\delta_{n}/4

(A.5)

for some
\delta_{n}\to 0
. Similarly, writing
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\cdot)
for the law under
g_{\eta}^{\prime}
,

W_{1}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(s_{j}),\,g_{\eta}^{\prime}*\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1}^{\prime},v_{j}\kappa_{2}^{\prime}+w_{j})\Big)\leq\delta_{n}/4.

(A.6)

We use
|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{1}^{\prime}|<c_{1}\Delta
and

|\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{2}^{\prime}|<c_{2}\max(\Delta,\Delta^{2})
for some sufficiently small

c_{1},c_{2}>0
to compare
these normal distributions. Note that for any two univariate normal laws,
by the coupling
(\mu+\sigma Z,\mu^{\prime}+\sigma^{\prime}Z)
for
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}))

\displaystyle\leq|\mu-\mu^{\prime}|+|\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}|\cdot\mathbb{E}[|Z|]

\displaystyle\leq|\mu-\mu^{\prime}|+C\min\Big(|\sigma^{2}-{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}|,|\sigma^{2}-{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}|^{1/2}\Big),

the second inequality holding when
\sigma^{2},{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}
are lower bounded by a
constant. By the definitions of
m_{j},v_{j}
and under Assumption
A
.3, we have
|m_{j}|=|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}|\leq C
,
v_{j}\leq\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{j}\leq C
, and
w_{j}=\sigma^{2}\|\mathbf{z}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\in[c,C]
for some constants
C,c>0
. Then for a sufficiently small choice of constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, we have

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j}),\,\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1}^{\prime},v_{j}\kappa_{2}^{\prime}+w_{j}))<\Delta/2.

Combining with (
A.6
), this implies

W_{1}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(s_{j}),\,g_{\eta}^{\prime}*\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j})\Big)\leq\delta_{n}/4+\Delta/2.

(A.7)

Then by (
A.5
), (
A.7
), and the triangle inequality and translation invariance for
W_{1}
,

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mathcal{L}(s_{j}),\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(s_{j}))

\displaystyle\geq W_{1}\Big(g_{\eta}*\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j}),\,g_{\eta}^{\prime}*\mathcal{N}(m_{j}\kappa_{1},v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j})\Big)-\delta_{n}/2-\Delta/2

\displaystyle=W_{1}\Big(g_{\sqrt{\eta^{2}+v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j}}},\,g_{\sqrt{\eta^{2}+v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j}}}^{\prime}\Big)-\delta_{n}/2-\Delta/2.

Recalling the choice of
v
in (
A.2
) and applying
\kappa_{2}\leq M^{2}+\eta^{2}
, we have
\eta^{2}+v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j}\leq v^{2}
. Then by monotonicity
of the smoothed Wasserstein distance in
the smoothing parameter
[
GG20
, Theorem 3]
,

W_{1}(g_{\sqrt{\eta^{2}+v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j}}},\,g_{\sqrt{\eta^{2}+v_{j}\kappa_{2}+w_{j}}}^{\prime})\geq W_{1}(g_{v},g_{v}^{\prime})=\Delta
, so

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mathcal{L}(s_{j}),\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(s_{j}))\geq\Delta/2-\delta_{n}/2.

Finally, this implies by the Kantorovich duality for
W_{1}
that there exists for each
j\in\mathcal{S}
a smooth 1-Lipschitz test function
f_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}[f_{j}(s_{j})]-\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[f_{j}(s_{j})]\geq(\Delta-\delta_{n})_{+}/3
.
We consider the test statistic

\displaystyle T(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}f_{j}(s_{j})=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}f_{j}(\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{\varepsilon}})),

which then satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}}[T(\mathbf{y})]-\mathbb{E}_{g_{\eta}^{\prime}}[T(\mathbf{y})]\geq cp(\Delta-\delta_{n})_{+}

because
|\mathcal{S}|\geq\alpha p
.
Computing the gradient in
({\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
, we see that

T(\mathbf{y})
is
L
-Lipschitz in
({\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
for

\displaystyle L^{2}=\bigg\lVert\mathbf{X}^{\top}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{z}_{j}\cdot f_{j}^{\prime}(s_{j})\bigg\rVert^{2}+\bigg\lVert\sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{z}_{j}\cdot f_{j}^{\prime}(s_{j})\bigg\rVert^{2}.

Applying
|f_{j}^{\prime}(s_{j})|\leq 1
for all
j\in\mathcal{S}
,
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{{\operatorname{op}}}\leq C_{*}
, and
the final condition of
Assumption
A
.4, this gives
L^{2}\leq Cp
.
Then (
A.3
) holds by the LSI for
({\bm{\theta}},{\bm{\varepsilon}})
, similarly
to Case 1.
∎

A direct consequence of Lemma
A.1
is the following pointwise comparison of the likelihood objective.

Corollary A.2
.

Let
v>0
and the sequence
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
be as in Lemma

A.1
. Fix any constant
\delta>0
. Then there exists some constants
c=c(\delta)
and
\tau=\tau(\delta)
such that for all large
n,p
and any
g\in\mathcal{P}(M)
with
d_{v}(g,g_{*})\geq\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{g_{*}}\left[\frac{1}{p}\Big(\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g_{*}}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\geq-\tau\right]\leq e^{-cp}.

Proof.

Writing as shorthand
P_{g}=P_{g}(\mathbf{y})
, Lemma
A.1
shows that for some constant
c>0
, all large
n,p
, and any

g\in\mathcal{P}(M)
with
d_{v}(g,g_{*})\geq\delta
, we have

\displaystyle e^{-cp\delta^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{g_{*}},P_{g})=\int\min(P_{g_{*}},P_{g})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\geq\int e^{-p\tau}P_{g_{*}}\mathbf{1}\{P_{g}\geq e^{-p\tau}P_{g_{*}}\}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}=e^{-p\tau}\mathbb{P}_{g_{*}}\left[p^{-1}\left(\log P_{g}-\log P_{g_{*}}\right)\geq{-}\tau\right]

for any
\tau>0
. The corollary follows upon choosing
\tau=c\delta^{2}/2
and adjusting constants.
∎

A.2
Covering net

We now define the following pseudo-metric

\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}
over
\mathcal{P}(M)
: Let
G_{\eta}(\cdot),G_{\eta}^{\prime}(\cdot)

denote the CDFs of
g_{\eta}=\mathcal{N}_{\eta}*g
and
g_{\eta}^{\prime}=\mathcal{N}_{\eta}*g^{\prime}
. (Here
G_{\eta}^{\prime}

is not to be confused with a derivative of
G_{\eta}
.) We set

\displaystyle\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime})=\max_{x:|x|\leq B}|G_{\eta}(x)-G_{\eta}^{\prime}(x)|.

Lemma A.3
.

Fix any constants
C_{0},\iota>0
. Let
\Pi_{X}
be the projection onto the column
span of
\mathbf{X}
. Then there exist constants

\eta,B,L>0
such that, on the event where
\|\Pi_{X}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C_{0}p
,
for any
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(M)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\Big|\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})\Big|\leq L\,\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime})+\iota.

Proof.

Let
[\mathbf{V}_{X}\mid\mathbf{W}_{X}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be an orthogonal matrix where

\Pi_{X}=\mathbf{V}_{X}\mathbf{V}_{X}^{\top}
and the columns of
\mathbf{V}_{X}
form an orthonormal basis for
the column span of
\mathbf{X}
.
Write
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{V}_{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{V}_{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
.
Then
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\theta}}+\mathbf{V}_{X}^{\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}
is independent of

\mathbf{W}_{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{W}_{X}^{\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}
, and
the law of the latter does not depend on the prior
g
. Thus

\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})=\log P_{g}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}})+\text{constant}

for a constant independent of
g
,
where
P_{g}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}})
denotes the marginal density of
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}
under
g
.
Observe that under Assumption
A
.1,

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\|_{{\operatorname{op}}}=\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\|_{{\operatorname{op}}}<C
for a constant
C>0
.
Then we may choose a constant
\eta>0
small enough such that
\eta\cdot\|\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\|_{\operatorname{op}}<\sigma^{2}/2
, and reparametrize the law of

\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}
equivalently as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}}+\widetilde{\bm{\varepsilon}},\qquad\varphi_{j}\overset{iid}{\sim}g_{\eta},\qquad\widetilde{\bm{\varepsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{\Sigma}}),

where
{\bm{\Sigma}}=\sigma^{2}\operatorname{Id}-\eta\,\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}
satisfies

(\sigma^{2}/2)\operatorname{Id}\preceq{\bm{\Sigma}}\preceq\sigma^{2}\operatorname{Id}
.
The marginal log-likelihood
\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})
then has the equivalent form

\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})=\log\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}})\right)\prod_{j=1}^{p}g_{\eta}(\varphi_{j})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}+\text{constant}

(A.8)

for a constant independent of
g
.

We now perform a change-of-measure from
G_{\eta}
to
G_{\eta}^{\prime}
by introducing

F_{\eta}(x)={G_{\eta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(G_{\eta}(x))
where
{G_{\eta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\cdot)
is the
functional inverse of the CDF
G_{\eta}^{\prime}(\cdot)
. (This is the univariate optimal
transport map from
G_{\eta}
to
G_{\eta}^{\prime}
.) Let us write
F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})
for
the coordinate-wise application of
F_{\eta}
to
{\bm{\varphi}}
. Then the marginal
log-likelihood
\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})
may also be written as

\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})=\log\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}))^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}))\right)\prod_{j=1}^{p}g_{\eta}(\varphi_{j})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}+\text{constant}.

(A.9)

Taking the difference of (
A.8
) and (
A.9
),

\displaystyle\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})=\log\left\langle e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}))^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}))+\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}})}\right\rangle,

where we introduce the shorthand

\langle f({\bm{\varphi}})\rangle=\frac{\int f({\bm{\varphi}})\,e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})}{\int e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})},\quad H({\bm{\varphi}})=\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}}),\quad\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})=\prod_{j=1}^{p}g_{\eta}(\varphi_{j})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}

for the expectation with respect to the posterior law of
{\bm{\varphi}}
under

g_{\eta}
given
(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}})
.

Let
C,c>0
denote constants changing from instance to instance, which may depend on
\eta
. By Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle-(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}))^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}))+(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}})\right\rangle.

Setting
\mathbf{u}=\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}{\bm{\varphi}}
and
\mathbf{v}=\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})
, we apply

\displaystyle\langle{-}\mathbf{v}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{u}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle{-}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})+2(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq-\langle(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})\rangle-2\langle(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v})\rangle^{1/2}\langle\mathbf{u}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\rangle^{1/2}.

Together with the conditions
{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}\preceq(2/\sigma^{2})\operatorname{Id}
,

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq C
under Assumption
A
.1, and
Cauchy-Schwarz, this gives

\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})\geq-C\left(\big\langle\|F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})-{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}^{2}\big\rangle+\big\langle\|F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})-{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}^{2}\big\rangle^{1/2}\big\langle H({\bm{\varphi}})\big\rangle^{1/2}\right).

(A.10)

To lower bound (
A.10
), we may first bound
\langle H({\bm{\varphi}})\rangle
as follows: Introduce the partition function

Z=\int e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})
.
Applying
H({\bm{\varphi}})\geq 0
,
e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}/Z\leq 1
on the event

e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}\leq Z
, and
H({\bm{\varphi}})<-\log Z
on the complementary event
e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}>Z
, we have

\langle H({\bm{\varphi}})\rangle=\int H({\bm{\varphi}})\frac{e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}}{Z}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})\\
\leq\int H({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})+\max(0,-\log Z)\leq 2\int H({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}),

the last step applying the Jensen’s inequality lower bound
\log Z\geq-\int H({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})
. Thus we have bounded the mean of
H({\bm{\varphi}})

under the posterior by its mean under the prior.
On the event
\|\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\Pi_{X}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C_{0}p
, applying

{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1}\preceq(2/\sigma^{2})\operatorname{Id}
,
\|\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq C
,
and that
g_{\eta}
is sub-Gaussian (since
g
has bounded support),
we have
\int H({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})\leq Cp
. This implies

\langle H({\bm{\varphi}})\rangle\leq Cp.

(A.11)

To bound
\langle\|F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})-{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle
, let us fix a small constant
t>0
to be determined, pick
B=B(t)>0
sufficiently large, and define

N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})=|\{j:|\varphi_{j}|>B\}|
. Applying again
\log Z\geq-\int H({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})\geq-Cp
and
H({\bm{\varphi}})\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{1}\{N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})>tp\}\rangle=\int\mathbf{1}\{N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})>tp\}\frac{e^{-H({\bm{\varphi}})}}{Z}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})\leq e^{Cp}\int\mathbf{1}\{N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})>tp\}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}}).

Under the product prior law
\prod_{j=1}^{p}g_{\eta}(\varphi_{j})
, this quantity

N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})
is a binomial random variable whose success probability is at
most
q=2e^{-cB^{2}}
for a constant
c:=c(M,\eta)
, by sub-Gaussianity of
g_{\eta}
. For
any fixed constant
t>0
, by choosing
B=B(t)>0
large enough, we have
q\leq t/2
, so by the Chernoff bound for the binomial distribution
[
BLM13
, pp. 24]

\displaystyle\int\mathbf{1}\{N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})>tp\}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})\leq\mathbb{P}_{g_{\eta}}\Big(\text{Bin}(p,q)-pq\geq\frac{t}{2}p\Big)\leq e^{-ph_{q}(q+\frac{t}{2})},

where
h_{q}(a)=\kl(\text{Ber}(a)||\text{Ber}(q))
. Since
h_{q}(q+t/2)\rightarrow\infty
as
q\downarrow 0
, we may then choose
B=B(t)

large enough such that
\int\mathbf{1}\{N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})>tp\}\mathrm{d}G_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})\leq e^{-2Cp}
. Consequently,

\langle\mathbf{1}\{N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})>tp\}\rangle\leq e^{Cp}\cdot e^{-2Cp}\leq e^{-Cp}
, and hence

\langle N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})\rangle\leq tp+p\cdot\langle\mathbf{1}\{N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})>tp\}\rangle\leq 2tp.

(A.12)

Now note that for all
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
, the smoothed distributions
g_{\eta}^{\prime}
have densities lower-bounded by a
\eta
-dependent constant on any fixed compact interval, and hence the inverse CDFs
{G_{\eta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\cdot)
are uniformly Lipschitz-continuous over any fixed compact sub-interval of
(0,1)
. Furthermore, for the above choice of
B=B(t)>0
, the interval
[G_{\eta}(-B),G_{\eta}(B)]
is contained in a common compact sub-interval of
(0,1)
across all
g\in\mathcal{P}(M)
.
Then there is a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(t)>0
such that for all
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
,
{G_{\eta}^{\prime}}^{-1}
is
C_{1}
-Lipschitz over the interval
[G_{\eta}(-B),G_{\eta}(B)]
. This shows that if
x\in[-B,B]
,

|F_{\eta}(x)-x|=|{G_{\eta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(G_{\eta}(x))-{G_{\eta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(G_{\eta}^{\prime}(x))|\leq C_{1}|G_{\eta}(x)-G_{\eta}^{\prime}(x)|\leq C_{1}\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime}).

(A.13)

Note that if
\theta\sim g
,
\theta^{\prime}\sim g^{\prime}
with
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(M)
and
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2})
are independent, then also

\mathbb{P}[\theta^{\prime}+\varepsilon\leq x-2M]\leq\mathbb{P}[\theta+\varepsilon\leq x]\leq\mathbb{P}[\theta^{\prime}+\varepsilon\leq x+2M]

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
because
|\theta-\theta^{\prime}|\leq 2M
. Then
G_{\eta}^{\prime}(x-2M)\leq G_{\eta}(x)\leq G_{\eta}^{\prime}(x+2M)
, implying that

|F_{\eta}(x)-x|=|{G_{\eta}^{\prime}}^{-1}(G_{\eta}(x))-x|\leq 2M

(A.14)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Combining these bounds (
A.13
) and
(
A.14
) and recalling
N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})=|\{j:|\varphi_{j}|>B\}|
which satisfies (
A.12
),

\Big\langle\|F_{\eta}({\bm{\varphi}})-{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}^{2}\Big\rangle\leq\Big\langle C_{1}^{2}\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime})^{2}\cdot p+(2M)^{2}\cdot N_{B}({\bm{\varphi}})\Big\rangle\leq(C_{1}^{2}\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime})^{2}+8M^{2}t)p.

(A.15)

We may apply (
A.11
) and (
A.15
) back to (
A.10
), now choosing the constant
t>0
sufficiently small based on the value of
\iota
given in the lemma, and then
B=B(t)
,
C_{1}=C_{1}(t)
, and
L=L(t)
sufficiently large, to obtain

\displaystyle\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})\geq-p\Big(L\cdot\max(\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime})^{2},\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime}))+\iota\Big).

This is simplified by observing that
\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(G,G^{\prime})\leq 1
by definition. Then, applying the same lower bound with
g,g^{\prime}
interchanged, we obtain as desired
|\log P_{g^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})|\leq p(L\cdot\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(g,g^{\prime})+\iota)
.
∎

A.3
Completing the proof

Corollary A.4
.

Fix any
\delta>0
. Then there exist constants
v,\tau>0
and
C,c>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{g_{*}}\left[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{P}(M):d_{v}(g,g_{*})\geq\delta}\frac{1}{p}\Big(\log P_{g}(\mathbf{y})-\log P_{g_{*}}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\geq-\tau\right]\leq Ce^{-cp}.

Proof.

In Lemma
A.3
, set
\iota=\tau/3
, and set
C_{0}>0
large
enough such that
\mathbb{P}_{g_{*}}[\|\Pi_{X}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}>C_{0}p]\leq e^{-cp}
. (This is
possible since
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq C
,
{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
has sub-Gaussian
coordinates under
g_{*}
, and
\|\Pi_{X}{\bm{\varepsilon}}\|_{2}^{2}\sim\chi_{k}^{2}
where

k=\rank(\mathbf{X})\leq p
.) Let
\eta,B,L
be as in the lemma. Take a
(\tau/3L)
-covering net of
\{g\in\mathcal{P}(M):d_{v}(g,g_{*})\geq\delta\}
in the pseudo-metric
\widetilde{d}_{\eta,B}(\cdot,\cdot)
, which by a standard packing argument has some finite cardinality
C(\eta,B,L,\tau)
because all CDFs
G_{\eta}
for
g\in\mathcal{P}(M)
have a constant lower bound for their derivatives over
[-B,B]
. The result then follows from Lemma
A.3
, combined with Corollary
A.2
applied to each point in this net with
-\tau/3
in place of
-\tau
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

By the Borel-Cantelli lemma, the above corollary implies that for any fixed
constant
\delta>0
, the estimator

\widetilde{g}
of Theorem
3.1
satisfies

d_{v}(\widetilde{g},g_{*})<\delta
almost surely for all large
n,p
.
Since
\delta>0
is arbitrary and this smoothed Wasserstein distance
d_{v}(\cdot,\cdot)
metrizes weak convergence, Theorem
3.1
follows.
∎

A.4
Assumption
A
for random designs

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Recall that
\Pi\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the projection orthogonal to
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For any
j\in[p]
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{j}=\Pi\mathbf{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\mathbf{b}_{j}-\mathbf{r}_{j},\qquad\mathbf{b}_{j}=\mathbf{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j},\qquad\mathbf{r}_{j}=\frac{\mathbf{X}\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}}{\lVert\mathbf{X}\bm{1}\rVert^{2}}\mathbf{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}.

Define the event

\displaystyle E=\Big\{

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{X}\rVert_{{\operatorname{op}}}\leq C_{0},\;\operatorname{Tr}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\geq c_{0}p,\;\|\mathbf{X}\bm{1}\|^{2}\geq c_{0}p,\;c_{0}\leq\min_{j\in[p]}\lVert\mathbf{b}_{j}\rVert^{2}\leq\max_{j\in[p]}\lVert\mathbf{b}_{j}\rVert^{2}\leq C_{0},

\displaystyle\max_{j\in[p]}|\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}-1|,\;\max_{j\neq k}|\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k}|\leq C_{0}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}},\;\;\max_{j\in[p]}\|\mathbf{r}_{j}\|\leq C_{0}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\Big)\Big\}

On this event
E
,
from these conditions and the decomposition
\mathbf{z}_{j}=\mathbf{b}_{j}-\mathbf{r}_{j}
, for a constant

C_{\beta}>0
we have

\max_{j\in[p]}\sum_{k:k\neq j}|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k}|^{2+\beta}\leq C_{\beta}p\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\Big)^{2+\beta}.

This approaches 0 as
n,p\to\infty
for
n/p\geq\gamma
and
any fixed
\beta,\gamma>0
. We have also

\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}=0
since
\Pi\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}=0
by definition of
\Pi
,
and it is direct to check the remaining
conditions of Assumption
A
.1–3 on this event
E
.
For Assumption
A
.4, observe that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}a_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{a}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{a}\leq\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert^{2}\cdot\bigg\lVert{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\Pi\mathbf{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\bigg\rVert_{{\operatorname{op}}}\leq Cp,

when
|a_{j}|\leq 1
for all
j\in[p]
and

\lVert\mathbf{X}\rVert_{{\operatorname{op}}}\leq C_{0}
. Thus Assumption
A
.4 holds
also on
E
.

Next we show that for some sufficiently large
C_{0}>0
and small
c_{0}>0
,
the event
E
holds with probability at least
1-p^{-10}
for
all large
n,p
. In the following,
C,C^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}>0
denote constants changing from
instance to instance. We have
\|\sqrt{n}\mathbf{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1/2}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq C(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})
with probability
1-e^{-cp}
, by

[
Ver18
, Theorem 4.6.1]
. Then, since
n/p\geq\gamma

and
\|{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq C
, this implies
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq C^{\prime}
.
For the remaining statements, observe
that for any (deterministic) unit vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, each of the products

\mathbf{X}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{X}\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has i.i.d.
(C/\sqrt{n})
-sub-Gaussian entries.
Then
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{v}
is a sum of

n
i.i.d.
(C^{\prime}/n)
-sub-exponential random variables, and Bernstein’s
inequality
[
Ver18
, Theorem 2.8.1]
implies

\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big|\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}\mathbf{v}\Big|\geq t\Big]=\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big|\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{v}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{v}\Big|\geq t\Big]\leq 2e^{-cn\min(t^{2},t)}.

Applying this with
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{e}_{j}
and taking a union bound over
j\in[p]
shows

\operatorname{Tr}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\geq cp
.
Applying this with
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{1}/\sqrt{p}

shows
\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}\|^{2}\geq cp
. Applying this with

\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}={\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}/\|{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}\|

shows
c\leq\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|^{2}\leq C
.
These statements all hold with probability at least
1-e^{-cn}\geq 1-p^{-20}
.

Furthermore, applying the above with

\mathbf{u}={\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}/\|{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}\|

and
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{e}_{j}
or
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{e}_{k}
shows
|\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}-1|\leq C_{0}\sqrt{\log p/n}
and
|\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{k}|\leq C_{0}\sqrt{\log p/n}

for any
j\neq k\in[p]
, with probability
1-e^{-cn\cdot C_{0}^{2}(\log p)/n}\geq 1-p^{-20}
for sufficiently large
C_{0}>0
. Applying
\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{1}\|^{2}\geq cp
already shown, we have
\|\mathbf{r}_{j}\|\leq(cp)^{-1/2}|\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}|
. Then applying the above with
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{1}/\sqrt{p}

and
\mathbf{v}={\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}/\|{\bm{\Sigma}}_{X}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}\|
shows

\|\mathbf{r}_{j}\|\leq C/\sqrt{p}+C_{0}\sqrt{\log p/n}
also with probability

1-e^{-cn\cdot C_{0}^{2}(\log p)/n}\geq 1-p^{-20}
.
Taking a union bound shows that for some constants
C_{0},c_{0}>0
, the event
E

holds with probability at least
1-p^{-10}
, as claimed. The proposition then
follows from the Borel-Cantelli lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Analysis of algorithm dynamics

B.1
Fisher-Rao flow for the sequence model

Proposition B.1
.

For any probability distributions
g,h
on
[-M,M]
,

\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g(\varphi)}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*h(\varphi)}\leq e^{(M^{2}/2\tau^{2})}e^{(2M/\tau^{2})|\varphi|}

Proof.

Observe that for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in[-M,M]
,

\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\theta-\varphi)}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\theta^{\prime}-\varphi)}=\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2\tau^{2}}(\theta-\varphi)^{2}}}{e^{-\frac{1}{2\tau^{2}}(\theta^{\prime}-\varphi)^{2}}}\in\left[e^{-\frac{M^{2}}{2\tau^{2}}-\frac{2M}{\tau^{2}}|\varphi|},\;e^{\frac{M^{2}}{2\tau^{2}}+\frac{2M}{\tau^{2}}|\varphi|}\right].

Then also

\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\theta-\varphi)}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*h(\varphi)}=\left(\int_{-M}^{M}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\theta^{\prime}-\varphi)}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\theta-\varphi)}\mathrm{d}h(\theta^{\prime})\right)^{-1}\leq e^{\frac{M^{2}}{2\tau^{2}}+\frac{2M}{\tau^{2}}|\varphi|},

and the result follows from further integrating this with respect to
\mathrm{d}g(\theta)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Assume that
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{0})<\infty
for otherwise there is nothing to prove.
By Corollary
C.2
,
\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\inf_{\theta\in[-M,M]}g_{t}(\theta)/g_{0}(\theta)\geq e^{-t}
.
As such,

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{0})+\int h\log\frac{g_{0}}{g_{t}}\leq D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{0})+t

which is finite for all
t
. Together with the weak lower semicontinuity of the KL divergence, this implies

\lim_{t\to 0}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{0}).

(B.1)

Next, write for simplicity
\bar{F}(g)=\bar{F}(g\mid\bar{q})
, and define its first
variation

\delta\bar{F}[g](\theta)=-\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{q}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g}(\theta)+1.

Applying Proposition
B.1
,

\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{q}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g}(\theta)\leq\int Ce^{C|\varphi|}\mathrm{d}\bar{q}(\varphi)<\infty
,
so
\delta\bar{F}[g]
is uniformly bounded over
\theta\in[-M,M]
.
Let
h
be any density on
[-M,M]
.
Applying the definition of the flow (
3.2
) and using this
boundedness to differentiate under the integral
by the dominated convergence theorem,

\int_{-M}^{M}{-}\delta\bar{F}[g_{t}](\theta)h(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=\int_{-M}^{M}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\log\frac{g_{t}(\theta)}{h(\theta)}\right)h(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t}).

(B.2)

By convexity of
x\mapsto{-}\log x
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{F}(g)-\bar{F}(h)

\displaystyle=\int\Big({-}\log[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g](\varphi)+\log[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*h](\varphi)\Big)\mathrm{d}\bar{q}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\leq\int\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\Big[\log[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*(g+\varepsilon(h-g))](\varphi)\Big]_{\varepsilon=0}\mathrm{d}\bar{q}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*(h-g)](\varphi)}{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g](\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\bar{q}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\theta-\varphi)h(\theta)}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g(\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\bar{q}(\varphi)-1=\int_{-M}^{M}{-}\delta\bar{F}[g](\theta)h(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta.

Combining the above two displays
and integrating from time
t
to time
T
yields

\int_{t}^{T}\big[\bar{F}(g_{s})-\bar{F}(h)\big]\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{t}^{T}{-}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})\Big|_{t=s}\mathrm{d}s=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})-D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{T}).

So taking the limit
t\to 0
above gives

\int_{0}^{T}\big[\bar{F}(g_{s})-\bar{F}(h)\big]\mathrm{d}s\leq D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{0})-D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{T})\leq D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{T}).

(B.3)

Now observe that

\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{-M}^{M}\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi-\theta)g_{t}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\right|=\left|\int_{-M}^{M}\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi-\theta)g_{t}(\theta)\delta\bar{F}[g_{t}](\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\right|\leq C\max_{\theta\in[-M,M]}\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi-\theta)

for a constant
C>0
,
using boundedness of
|\delta\bar{F}[g]|
as shown above to
differentiate under the integral. Then

\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}](\varphi)\right|=\left|\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{-M}^{M}\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi-\theta)g_{t}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}(\varphi)}\right|\leq Ce^{C|\varphi|}

by Proposition
B.1
, so again
differentiating under the integral by the dominated convergence theorem
and applying
\int g(\theta)\delta\bar{F}[g](\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bar{F}(g_{t})

\displaystyle=\int{-}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}](\varphi)\mathrm{d}\bar{q}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int{-}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(\varphi-\theta)g_{t}(\theta)\delta\bar{F}[g_{t}](\theta)}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}(\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\bar{q}(\varphi)={-}\int\Big(\delta\bar{F}[g_{t}](\theta)\Big)^{2}g_{t}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta\leq 0,

(B.4)

i.e.
\bar{F}
is non-increasing along the gradient flow path. Thus

T(\bar{F}(g_{T})-\bar{F}(h))\leq\int_{0}^{T}[\bar{F}(g_{s})-\bar{F}(h)]\mathrm{d}s\leq D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{0})

and rearranging yields the theorem.
∎

B.2
LSI for Langevin dynamics in high noise

Proof of Theorem
3.4
-(1).

Recall that
{\bm{\varphi}}={\bm{\theta}}+\mathbf{z}
where
\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\operatorname{Id})

and
\tau^{2}+\delta<\sigma^{2}/\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\operatorname{op}}^{2}
.
Using also
M^{2}+\delta<\sigma^{2}/\lVert\mathbf{X}\rVert_{{\operatorname{op}}}^{2}
, we may pick
some constant
{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}\in(\min(M^{2},\tau^{2}),\sigma^{2}/\lVert\mathbf{X}\rVert_{{\operatorname{op}}}^{2})

depending only on
(M,\tau,\delta)
. Then, defining

\eta^{2}={\tau^{\prime}}^{2}-\tau^{2}>0
and
{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}={\bm{\varphi}}+\mathbf{z}^{\prime}={\bm{\theta}}+\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
for

\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2}\operatorname{Id})
independent of
\mathbf{z}
, we have the equivalent
representation
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}
with
{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\prime})

independent of
{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}
such that

{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\prime}=\sigma^{2}\operatorname{Id}-{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}
is positive definite.

Write as shorthand
\nu({\bm{\varphi}})=P_{g}({\bm{\varphi}}\mid\mathbf{y})
,

\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}({\bm{\varphi}})=P_{g}({\bm{\varphi}}\mid{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})
,
and
\mu({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})=P_{g}({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{y})
. Following the decomposition of

[
BB19
]
,
for any bounded, Lipschitz, and
continuously differentiable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, by the
tower property

\displaystyle\ent_{\nu}(f^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}f^{2}\log f^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\nu}f^{2}\cdot\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu}f^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}\sim\mu}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}f^{2}\log f^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}f^{2}\cdot\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}f^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}\sim\mu}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}f^{2}\cdot\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}f^{2}\Big]-\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}f^{2}]\cdot\log\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}f^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\ent_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})+\ent_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2}).

(B.5)

For the first term, note that
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}({\bm{\varphi}})
is a product measure
with marginals

\displaystyle\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}(\varphi_{j})\propto P_{g}(\varphi_{j}\mid\varphi_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\propto e^{-\frac{(\varphi_{j}^{\prime}-\varphi_{j})^{2}}{2\eta^{2}}}\cdot\int e^{-\frac{(\varphi_{j}-\theta)^{2}}{2\tau^{2}}}\mathrm{d}g(\theta)\propto\int e^{-\frac{(\varphi_{j}-c(\theta))^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{g}(\theta),

(B.6)

where

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}=\frac{\eta^{2}\tau^{2}}{\eta^{2}+\tau^{2}},\quad c(\theta)=\frac{\tau^{2}\varphi_{j}^{\prime}+\eta^{2}\theta}{\eta^{2}+\tau^{2}},\quad\mathrm{d}\bar{g}(\theta)=\frac{e^{-\frac{(\theta-\varphi_{j}^{\prime})^{2}}{2(\eta^{2}+\tau^{2})}}\mathrm{d}g(\theta)}{\int e^{-\frac{(u-\varphi_{j}^{\prime})^{2}}{2(\eta^{2}+\tau^{2})}}\mathrm{d}g(u)}.

(B.7)

In other words,
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}
is the convolution between
\mathcal{N}(0,\gamma^{2})
and the pushforward of
\bar{g}
under
c
, which is supported on an interval of length at most
2M
. By
[
Zim16
, Theorem 1]
,

\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}
satisfies a one-dimensional LSI with constant
C=C(\tau,\tau^{\prime},M)
:

\displaystyle\ent_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}}(f^{2})\leq C\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}}|\partial_{j}f({\bm{\varphi}})|^{2}

(B.8)

where we write

\ent_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}}
for the partial
entropy and expectation over the coordinate
\varphi_{j}
of
{\bm{\varphi}}\sim\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}
.
Finally, by tensorization of LSI
[
Vil09
, Theorem 9.9(i)]
, the product measure
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}
satisfies a LSI with the same
C
, namely,

\displaystyle\ent_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})\leq C\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}\lVert\nabla f({\bm{\varphi}})\rVert^{2}.

(B.9)

Taking further expectation under
\mu
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\ent_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})\leq C\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\lVert\nabla f({\bm{\varphi}})\rVert^{2}.

(B.10)

To bound the second term in (
B.2
), note that

\mu({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})\propto\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})^{\top}{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\varphi^{\prime})-\sum_{j=1}^{p}V(\varphi_{j}^{\prime})\big)
, where

\displaystyle V(\varphi^{\prime})={-}\log\int e^{-\frac{1}{2{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}(\varphi^{\prime}-\theta)^{2}}\mathrm{d}g(\theta).

We have

\displaystyle V^{\prime\prime}(\varphi^{\prime})=\frac{1}{{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}-\partial_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{2}\log\int e^{\frac{\varphi^{\prime}\theta}{{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}}\mathrm{d}g(\theta)=\frac{1}{{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}-\frac{\var_{g}(\theta\mid\varphi^{\prime})}{{\tau^{\prime}}^{4}}\geq\frac{1}{{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}-\frac{M^{2}}{{\tau^{\prime}}^{4}}=:c(\tau^{\prime},M)>0,

using the fact that
P(\theta\mid\varphi^{\prime})
is supported on
[-M,M]
and

{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}>M^{2}
. Hence the density of
\mu({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})
is strictly log-concave,
so by the Bakry-Emery criterion
[
Vil09
, Theorem 9.9(iii)]
,
\mu({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})

satisfies a LSI with constant
c(\tau^{\prime},M)^{-1}
. Let
G({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})\equiv[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})]^{1/2}
. Then

\displaystyle\ent_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})=\ent_{\mu}(G^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}))\leq c(\tau^{\prime},M)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\lVert\nabla G({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})\rVert^{2}.

(B.11)

We have

\displaystyle\partial_{\varphi_{i}^{\prime}}G(\bm{\varphi}^{\prime})=\frac{\partial_{\varphi_{i}^{\prime}}G^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})}{2G({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{2\eta^{2}}\frac{\cov_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2},\varphi_{i})}{[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})]^{1/2}}=\frac{1}{2\eta^{2}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},-i}}\cov_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2},\varphi_{i})}{[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})]^{1/2}},

(B.12)

where
the second equality follows from
\partial_{\varphi_{i}^{\prime}}G^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}[f^{2}\partial_{\varphi_{i}^{\prime}}\log P_{g}(\varphi_{i}|\varphi_{i}^{\prime})]
and
the fact that
\partial_{\varphi_{i}^{\prime}}\log P_{g}(\varphi_{i}|\varphi_{i}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}(\varphi_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{i}|\varphi^{\prime}_{i}])
from (
B.6
), and
the last equality uses the fact that
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}
is a product
measure.
Shorthand
\cov_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2},\varphi_{i})
as
\cov_{i}(f^{2},\varphi_{i}\mid\varphi_{i}^{\prime})
. Note that (
B.6
) yields the
representation of the conditional law

(\varphi_{i}\mid\varphi_{i}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}c(\theta)+z

where
c(\theta)
is given in (
B.7
), and

\theta\sim\bar{g}(\cdot)
and
z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\gamma^{2})
are independent
(conditional on
\varphi_{i}^{\prime}
). Consequently,

\displaystyle\cov_{i}(f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}),\varphi_{i}\mid\varphi_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\cov_{i}(f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta)+z),c(\theta)+z\mid\varphi_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\cov_{i}(f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta)+z),c(\theta)\mid\varphi_{i}^{\prime})+\cov_{i}(f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta)+z),z\mid\varphi_{i}^{\prime})\equiv(I)+(II).

To bound
(I)
, let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\sim\bar{g}(\cdot)
and
z_{1},z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\gamma^{2})
be independent. Then

\displaystyle 2\cdot(I)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[(f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{1})+z_{1})-f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{2})+z_{2}))(c(\theta_{1})-c(\theta_{2}))\;\Big|\;\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{1})+z_{1})-f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{2})+z_{2})\Big)^{2}\;\Big|\;\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\Big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{1})+z_{1})+f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{2})+z_{2})\Big)^{2}(c(\theta_{1})-c(\theta_{2}))^{2}\;\Big|\;\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\Big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=(2\var_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}[f])^{1/2}\cdot\frac{\eta^{2}}{{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{1})+z_{1})+f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta_{2})+z_{2})\Big)^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}\;\Big|\;\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\Big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C(M,\tau,\tau^{\prime})\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(\partial_{i}f)^{2}\big]^{1/2}\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2})\big]^{1/2},

where the last step applies the Poincaré inequality for

\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}
(as implied by its LSI (
B.8
) – see
[
Vil09
, (9.32)]
) to
\var_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}[f]
, and the
fact that
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in[-M,M]
. To bound
(II)
, using the independence of

z
and
\theta
and applying Stein’s lemma,

\displaystyle(II)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta)+z)z\;\Big|\;\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\Big]=2\gamma^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta)+z)\partial_{i}f({\bm{\varphi}}_{-i},c(\theta)+z)\;\Big|\;\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(\partial_{i}f)^{2}\big]^{1/2}\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2})\big]^{1/2}.

Combining the two bounds and applying Cauchy-Schwarz and the tower property
for
\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},-i}}
yields

\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},-i}}\cov_{i}(f^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}),\varphi_{i}\mid\varphi_{i}^{\prime})\leq C\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(\partial_{i}f)^{2}\big]^{1/2}\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})\big]^{1/2}.

Substituting this into (
B.11
) and (
B.12
),
the second term in (
B.2
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\ent_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(\partial_{i}f)^{2}=C^{\prime}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\lVert\nabla f\rVert^{2}

(B.13)

for some
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\tau,\tau^{\prime},M)
. Combining (
B.10
) and (
B.13
) concludes the proof.
∎

The proof of Theorem
3.4
-(2) requires the following
additional lemma, whose proof we present after the main proof.

Lemma B.2
.

Fix any
M>0
. Uniformly over
h\in\mathbb{R}
, the measure
\mu_{h}(x)\propto e^{hx}\bm{1}_{|x|<M}
satisfies a LSI over
(-M,M)
with constant
C=C(M)>0
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
-(2).

The proof is similar to Theorem
3.4
-(1), and we skip some of the
common steps. Pick
{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}\in(M^{2},\sigma^{2}/\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\operatorname{op}}^{2})

depending only on
(M,\delta)
.
Let
{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}={\bm{\theta}}+\mathbf{z}
with
\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}\operatorname{Id})
, and
represent

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}
with
{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\prime})

and
{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\prime}=\sigma^{2}-{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top}
.
Then, denoting
\nu=P_{g}({\bm{\theta}}\mid\mathbf{y})
,
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}=P_{g}({\bm{\theta}}\mid{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})
and
\mu=P_{g}({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{y})
, we have, analogous to (
B.2
),

\ent_{\nu}(f^{2})=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\ent_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})+\ent_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2}).

Here
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}
is a product measure with marginals

\displaystyle\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}(\theta_{j})\propto e^{-\frac{\varphi_{j}^{\prime}}{\tau^{2}}\theta_{j}}\cdot e^{-\frac{\theta_{j}^{2}}{2\tau^{2}}}g(\theta_{j}).

By Lemma
B.2
, the density proportional to

e^{-\frac{\varphi_{j}^{\prime}}{\tau^{2}}\theta_{j}}
on
(-M,M)
satisfies a LSI
with constant
C=C(M)>0
uniformly over
\varphi_{j}^{\prime}
. Then by the
Holley-Stroock perturbation principle
[
Vil09
, Theorem 9.9(ii)]
,
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},j}
also satisfies a LSI
with constant
C=C^{\prime}(M,\tau)\kappa(g)^{2}
.
Applying this and tensorization shows

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\ent_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})\leq C\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\lVert\nabla f({\bm{\theta}})\rVert^{2}.

For the second term, we have again that
\mu
is strongly log-concave
and hence satisfies a LSI with some constant
c(\tau^{\prime},M)^{-1}<\infty
.
Let
G({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})\equiv[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})]^{1/2}
. Then as before,

\displaystyle\ent_{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})=\ent_{\mu}(G^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}))\leq c(\tau^{\prime},M)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\lVert\nabla G({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})\rVert^{2},

where

\displaystyle\partial_{\varphi_{i}^{\prime}}G({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})=\frac{\partial_{\varphi_{i}^{\prime}}G^{2}({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})}{2G({\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{2{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},-i}}\cov_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2},\theta_{i})}{[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(f^{2})]^{1/2}}.

Here, since
\theta_{i}\in[-M,M]
is bounded, we have simply

\displaystyle\cov_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2},\theta_{i})

\displaystyle\leq C(M)\cdot\var_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2})\leq C^{\prime}(M)\cdot\big[\var_{\nu_{\varphi^{\prime},i}}(f)\big]^{1/2}\cdot\big[\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}}(f^{2})\big]^{1/2}.

Then, applying again the Poincaré inequality for
\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime},i}
gives

\ent_{\mu}\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}(f^{2})\leq C\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{{\bm{\varphi}}^{\prime}}}(\partial_{i}f)^{2}=C\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\lVert\nabla f\rVert^{2}

which completes the proof.
∎

To prove Lemma
B.2
, we use the following characterization of
the LSI constant due to
[
BG99
, Theorem 5.3]
. We note that
this result has also been applied in
[
ZQM11
]
to prove an analogue of Lemma
B.2
under spherical priors and general dimensions.

Lemma B.3

(
[
BG99
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a (Borel) probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
and let
\rho(x)
be the
density of its absolutely continuous part with respect to Lebesgue measure.
Let
m
be a median of
\mu
. Then the optimal constant
C_{\ast}
in the LSI

\displaystyle\ent_{\mu}(f^{2})\leq C_{*}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[{f^{\prime}}^{2}]

satisfies
c_{1}\max(B_{-},B_{+})\leq C_{\ast}\leq c_{2}\max(B_{-},B_{+})
for some universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}
, where

\displaystyle B_{+}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x>m}\mu\big([x,\infty)\big)\cdot\log\frac{1}{\mu\big([x,\infty)\big)}\cdot\int_{m}^{x}\frac{1}{\rho(t)}\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle B_{-}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x>m}\mu\big((-\infty,x]\big)\cdot\log\frac{1}{\mu\big((-\infty,x]\big)}\cdot\int_{x}^{m}\frac{1}{\rho(t)}\mathrm{d}t.

This result does not require
\mu
to have full support on all of
\mathbb{R}
, and

B_{+}
or
B_{-}
is defined as 0 if
\mu((m,\infty))=0
or
\mu((-\infty,m))=0

respectively.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

The uniform density on
(-M,M)
satisfies a LSI with constant
C(M)>0
by

[
GMW14
]
. Then by the Holley-Stroock perturbation principle, it
suffices to show the result for
|h|>h_{0}(M)
and any fixed and large
constant
h_{0}(M)>0
. By symmetry, we may assume
h>0
.
For presentational simplicity, we only prove the case
M=1
, and the case of general
M
follows similarly.

Using Lemma
B.3
, it suffices to control the
quantities
B_{+},B_{-}
for
\mu_{h}
.
Let us write
a(h)\asymp b(h)
if there exist constants
C,c>0
for which

ca(h)\leq b(h)\leq Ca(h)
for all large
h
.
Let
m\equiv m(h)
be the median of
\mu_{h}
, and let

\rho_{h}(t)=e^{ht}/(h^{-1}(e^{h}-e^{-h}))
be the density of
\mu_{h}
.
We claim that
1-m\asymp\frac{1}{h}
.
To see this, note that for any
x\in(-1,1)
,

\displaystyle\mu_{h}([-1,x])=\int_{-1}^{x}\frac{e^{ht}}{h^{-1}(e^{h}-e^{-h})}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{e^{hx}-e^{-h}}{e^{h}-e^{-h}},

so for sufficiently large
h
,
\inf\{x:\mu_{h}([-1,x])\geq 1/2\}\in[1-C/h,1-c/h]
.

We first bound
B_{+}
. Using the boundedness of
t\mapsto t\log(1/t)
and

\displaystyle\int_{m}^{x}\frac{1}{\rho_{h}(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h^{2}}(e^{h}-e^{-h})(e^{-hm}-e^{-hx})\asymp\frac{1}{h^{2}}(e^{h(1-m)}-e^{h(1-x)}),

we have for
1-m\asymp\frac{1}{h}
and any
x\in(m,1)
that

\displaystyle\mu_{h}\big([x,1]\big)\cdot\log\frac{1}{\mu_{h}\big([x,1]\big)}\cdot\int_{m}^{x}\frac{1}{\rho_{h}(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{C}{h^{2}}

for a constant
C>0
independent of
x
. Then
B_{+}\leq C/h^{2}
.

Next we bound
B_{-}
. Using

\displaystyle\mu_{h}\big([-1,x]\big)=\frac{e^{hx}-e^{-h}}{e^{h}-e^{-h}}\asymp e^{-h(1-x)}(1-e^{-h(1+x)}),\quad\int_{x}^{m}\frac{1}{\rho_{h}(t)}\mathrm{d}t\asymp\frac{e^{h(1-x)}}{h^{2}}(1-e^{h(x-m)}),

we have for any
x\in(-1,m)
and a constant
C>0
that

\displaystyle\mu_{h}\big([-1,x]\big)\cdot\log\frac{1}{\mu_{h}\big([-1,x])\big)}\cdot\int_{x}^{m}\frac{1}{\rho_{h}(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{h^{2}}(1-e^{-h(1+x)})\log\frac{e^{h}-e^{-h}}{e^{hx}-e^{-h}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{h^{2}}(1-e^{-h(1+x)})\log\frac{e^{h(1-x)}}{1-e^{-h(1+x)}}.

Applying again boundedness of
t\mapsto t\log(1/t)
, we have in this case

B_{-}\leq C^{\prime}/h
for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
.
In summary,
\max(B_{+},B_{-})
is at most a constant,
which in light of Lemma
B.3
completes the proof.
∎

B.3
Analysis of the joint gradient flow

We isolate the following intuitive but somewhat
technical lemma, which verifies the statement

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})=-p\|\operatorname{grad}_{q}^{W_{2}}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})\|_{q_{t}}^{2}-\alpha\|\operatorname{grad}_{g}^{{\operatorname{FR}}}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})\|_{g_{t}}^{2}

where
\|\cdot\|_{q_{t}}^{2},\|\cdot\|_{g_{t}}^{2}
are the squared norms under the
Wasserstein-2 metric tensor at
q_{t}
and Fisher-Rao metric tensor at
g_{t}
,
respectively. (For background on these metric tensors, see Section

D.1
.)

Lemma B.4
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3.7
,

\limsup_{t\to 0}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})\leq F_{n}(q_{0},g_{0}).

Furthermore
t\mapsto F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})
is differentiable at every
t>0
, and

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})=-\frac{1}{p}\int\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\mathrm{d}q_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\nu[g_{t}]}\right\|_{2}^{2}q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}-\alpha\int\bigg(\bigg[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{q}_{t}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}}\bigg](\theta)-1\bigg)^{2}g_{t}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta.

(B.14)

The proof of Lemma
B.4
is deferred to Appendix

C.4
.

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.

Assume
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{0})<\infty
, as otherwise there is nothing to prove.
Then by Theorem
3.6
-(1) and the same argument leading to
(
B.1
),
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})
is finite for all
t
and continuous at
t=0
. Next, recall that
{-}\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g_{t}](\theta)=\left(\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{\nu}[g_{t}]}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}}\right)(\theta)-1
, so for any density
h
supported on
[-M,M]
,

\int_{-M}^{M}-\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g_{t}](\theta)\cdot h(\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*h}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}}(\varphi)\bar{\nu}[g_{t}](\varphi)-1=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*h}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}}(\varphi_{j})\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})-1.

(B.15)

Denote

f_{t}(\varphi)=\log\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*h}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}}(\varphi),\qquad\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})=\int f_{t}(\varphi_{j})\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}}).

Then

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*h}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}}(\varphi_{j})\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})

\displaystyle=\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})}\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})

\displaystyle=\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})}q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})+e^{\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})}\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})}(\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})-q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})).

(B.16)

Observe that
f_{t}(\varphi_{j})
is
2M
-Lipschitz in
{\bm{\varphi}}
, because

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}f_{t}(\varphi)=\frac{(\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast h)^{\prime}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast h}(\varphi)-\frac{(\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast g_{t})^{\prime}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast g_{t}}(\varphi)=\mathbb{E}_{h}[\theta\mid\theta+\tau Z=\varphi]-\mathbb{E}_{g_{t}}[\theta\mid\theta+\tau Z=\varphi]\in[-2M,2M]

with
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Then for some
(M,\tau)
-dependent constants
C,C^{\prime},c>0
,

f_{t}(\varphi_{j})
is
C^{\prime}
-subgaussian under the law
\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})

by the given LSI assumption (cf.
[
Vil09
, Theorem 9.9(iv)]
),
so for any
L>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})>L}e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})}(\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})-q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}}))

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\leq\int_{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})>L}e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})}\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})\leq Ce^{-cL^{2}}.

(B.17)

Next, applying
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\nu[g_{t}],q_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\int|\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})-q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})|

and Pinsker’s inequality
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\nu[g_{t}],q_{t})\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q_{t}\|\nu[g_{t}])}
, we have

\int_{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})\leq L}e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})}(\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})-q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}}))\leq 2e^{L}d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\nu[g_{t}],q_{t})\leq e^{L}\sqrt{2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q_{t}\|\nu[g_{t}])}.

(B.18)

Applying (
B.14
) without
the second term as an upper bound,
and applying again the LSI for
\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})
with

f=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q_{t}/\mathrm{d}\nu[g_{t}]}
in its definition (
3.9
),
we have for a constant
c:=c(M,\tau)>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{p}\int\bigg\lVert\nabla\log\frac{\mathrm{d}q_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\nu[g_{t}]}\bigg\rVert_{2}^{2}q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\leq-\frac{c}{p}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q_{t}\|\nu[g_{t}])\leq 0.

(B.19)

Applying (
B.19
) to (
B.18
) and combining with
(
B.17
), we obtain for
(M,\tau)
-dependent constants
C_{0},c_{0}>0
that

\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})-\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})}(\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})-q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}}))\leq C_{0}\left(e^{-c_{0}L^{2}}+e^{L}\sqrt{-p\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})}\right):=H_{t}(L)

(B.20)

where we define
H_{t}(L)
to be this (non-negative) upper bound.

Now by Jensen’s inequality,

e^{\nu_{j}[g_{t}](f_{t})}\leq\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})}\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})
.
Applying this and (
B.20
) back to (
B.16
),

\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})}\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}})-1\leq\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})}q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})-1+H_{t}(L)\int e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})}\nu[g_{t}]({\bm{\varphi}}).

Then, averaging over
j=1,\ldots,p
, applying the identity
(
B.15
), and using

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{f_{t}(\varphi_{j})}q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})-1=\int\frac{\mathcal{N}*h}{\mathcal{N}*g_{t}}(\varphi)\bar{q}_{t}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\varphi-1=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}[-D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})]

(where we may differentiate under the integral by the same
argument as in (
B.2
)), we get

\int_{-M}^{M}-\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g_{t}](\theta)\cdot h(\theta)\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}[-D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})]+H_{t}(L)\Big(\int_{-M}^{M}-\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g_{t}](\theta)\cdot h(\theta)+1\Big).

This yields the implication

\int_{-M}^{M}-\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g_{t}](\theta)\cdot h(\theta)\geq 0\text{ and }H_{t}(L)\leq 1/2\Longrightarrow\int-\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g_{t}](\theta)\cdot h(\theta)\leq 2H_{t}(L)+2\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}[{-}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h\|g_{t})].

Now, suppose by contradiction that
