\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\int_{-M}^{M}|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}g_{t}(\theta)|\mathrm{d}\theta<\infty

for each
a\leq r
, it is direct to check
that each coordinate of
(t,{\bm{\varphi}})\mapsto\nabla U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})
belongs to

H_{\text{loc}}^{r,\infty}
. Then (
C.24
) yields the implications

q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\in H_{\text{loc}}^{0,1}\Rightarrow q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\in H_{\text{loc}}^{0,2}\Rightarrow q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\in H_{\text{loc}}^{0,3}\Rightarrow\ldots
where the initial condition comes from
(
C.22
), hence
q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\in H_{\text{loc}}^{0,\infty}
.
Then (
C.24
) further yields the implications

q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\in H_{\text{loc}}^{0,\infty}\Rightarrow q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\in H_{\text{loc}}^{1,\infty}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\in H_{\text{loc}}^{r,\infty}
. Finally, by the
Sobolev embedding theorem (cf.
[
AF03
, Theorem 4.2 Part II]
) we have

H_{\text{loc}}^{r,\infty}\subset C^{r-1,\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{p})
.
∎

C.3
Completing the proof

We are now ready to prove Theorem
3.6
.

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

We start by showing the existence and uniqueness of a solution of
(
2.15
–
2.16
) on
[0,t_{0}]
for some small
t_{0}
to be
specified later.
For the given initialization
q_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
, let
K>0
be such that
(
C.14
) holds with
K(q_{0})=K
, and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{q}^{\prime}(t_{0})=\{q\in\mathcal{L}_{q}(t_{0}):\max_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}\max_{j\in[p]}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\sim q_{t}}e^{(8M/\tau^{2})|\varphi_{t,j}|}\leq K\}.

(C.25)

Observe that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}(t_{0})
is closed in
\mathcal{L}_{q}(t_{0})
. Indeed,
since
d_{W}
metrizes weak convergence, for any sequence
q_{n}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}(t_{0})
converging to
q\in\mathcal{L}_{q}(t_{0})
, it holds that

q_{n}\Rightarrow q
weakly, and thus

q_{n,t}\Rightarrow q_{t}
weakly for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. Then by Fatou’s lemma,

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\sim q_{t}}e^{(8M/\tau^{2})|\varphi_{t,j}|}\leq\liminf_{n}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\sim q_{n,t}}e^{(8M/\tau^{2})|\varphi_{t,j}|}\leq K(q_{0})
.
This shows that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}(t_{0})
is closed, and hence also complete.

Define a map
\Gamma:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0})\to\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0})
where

\Gamma(q,g)=(\Gamma_{q}(g),\Gamma_{g}(q))
, as follows:

•

\Gamma_{q}(g)
is the law of the unique strong solution to
(
C.10
) defined by
g
, with initialization
q_{0}
,
as guaranteed by Lemma
C.3
-(1). Here,
Lemma
C.3
-(2) ensures that

\Gamma_{q}(g)\in\mathcal{L}_{q}^{\prime}(t_{0})
.

•

\Gamma_{g}(q)
is the
unique solution to (
C.3
) defined by
q
, with initialization
g_{0}
,
as guaranteed by Lemma
C.1
-(1).

Equip
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0})
with the metric

\displaystyle d_{\ast}((q,g),(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{g}))=d_{W}(q,\widetilde{q})+d_{\infty,\mathrm{TV}}(g,\widetilde{g}).

Since both
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}
and
\mathcal{L}_{g}
are complete metric spaces,

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}\times\mathcal{L}_{g}
is also complete.
Moreover, by Lemmas
C.1
-(2) and

C.3
-(3), for any
q,\widetilde{q}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{q}(t_{0})
and

g,\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0})
, there exist
C_{0},T_{0}>0
depending on

K
for which

d_{\ast}(\Gamma(q,g),\Gamma(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{g}))\leq C_{0}t_{0}\cdot d_{\ast}\big((q,g),(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{g})\big)

for any
t_{0}\leq T_{0}
. Then, choosing
t_{0}
to satisfy also

t_{0}\leq 1/2C_{0}
, we obtain that
\Gamma
is a contraction in
d_{\ast}
.
By the Banach fixed point theorem, there
is a unique fixed point
(q^{\ast},g^{\ast})\in\mathcal{L}_{q}^{\prime}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0})
of
\Gamma
.

By Lemma
C.1
-(1),
g^{\ast}
is a (classical) solution to
(
2.16
) defined by
q^{\ast}
and the initial condition
g_{0}
, and
by Lemma
C.3
-(1),
q^{\ast}
is a weak solution to
(
C.11
) defined by
g^{\ast}
with the initial condition

q_{0}
, in the sense (
C.12
).
Suppose inductively that

t\mapsto g_{t}^{\ast}(\theta)
belongs to
C^{r}((0,t_{0}))

for each fixed
\theta\in[-M,M]
and that
\sup_{t\in(0,t_{0})}\int_{-M}^{M}|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}g_{t}^{\ast}(\theta)|\mathrm{d}\theta<\infty

for all
a=0,\ldots,r
. Then Lemma
C.4
-(1) shows
that
t\mapsto f_{t}(\theta)=\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{q}_{t}^{\ast}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}^{\ast}}(\theta)
belongs also to
C^{r}((0,t_{0}))

and
\sup_{t\in(0,t_{0})}\sup_{\theta\in[-M,M]}|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}f_{t}(\theta)|<\infty
for each
a=0,\ldots,r
. Then

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r+1}}g_{t}(\theta)=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r}}[g_{t}(\theta)(f_{t}(\theta)-1)]
is continuous in
t
,
and satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,t_{0})}\int_{-M}^{M}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r+1}}g_{t}(\theta)\right|\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in(0,t_{0})}\int_{-M}^{M}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r}}[g_{t}(\theta)(f_{t}(\theta)-1)]\right|\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in(0,t_{0})}\sum_{a=0}^{r}\binom{r}{a}\sup_{\theta\in[-M,M]}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r-a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r-a}}(f_{t}(\theta)-1)\right|\int_{-M}^{M}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}g_{t}(\theta)\right|\mathrm{d}\theta<\infty.

Thus the inductive claim holds for
r+1
. Then

t\mapsto g_{t}^{\ast}(\theta)
belongs to
C^{\infty}((0,t_{0}))
for each fixed

\theta
, and
\sup_{t\in(0,t_{0})}\int_{-M}^{M}|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r}}g_{t}^{\ast}(\theta)|\mathrm{d}\theta<\infty
for each fixed
r\geq 0
.
Then Lemma
C.4
-(2) implies that
(t,{\bm{\varphi}})\mapsto q_{t}^{\ast}({\bm{\varphi}})
belongs to
C^{\infty}((0,t_{0})\times\mathbb{R}^{p})
.
Applying this smoothness of
q_{t}^{\ast}({\bm{\varphi}})
and
integrating (
C.12
) by parts, we obtain that
q^{\ast}
satisfies
(
2.15
) pointwise at each
(t,{\bm{\varphi}})\in(0,t_{0})\times\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
Furthermore, the uniform boundedness and continuity of
q_{t}^{\ast}({\bm{\varphi}})
and
weak convergence
q_{t}^{\ast}\Rightarrow q_{0}^{\ast}
imply that
q_{t}^{\ast}({\bm{\varphi}})\to q_{0}^{\ast}({\bm{\varphi}})
pointwise over
{\bm{\varphi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, so
\{q_{t}^{\ast}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
is a classical solution to (
2.15
).

Conversely, for any solution
(q,g)
to (
2.16
–
2.15
),
Lemma
C.3
-(2) applied
with this
g
shows that we must have
q\in\mathcal{L}_{q}^{\prime}(t_{0})
. Then
(q,g)\in\mathcal{L}_{q}^{\prime}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0})
is also a fixed point of

\Gamma
, so by the uniqueness guarantees in Lemmas

C.1
-(1) and
C.3
-(1),

q=q^{\ast}
and
g=g^{\ast}
as equalities of elements in

\mathcal{L}_{q}^{\prime}(t_{0})
and
\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0})
. Since
(q,g)
are continuous,
this implies
g_{t}(\theta)=g^{\ast}_{t}(\theta)
and

q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})=q^{\ast}_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})
pointwise,
so the solution
(q^{\ast},g^{\ast})
to (
2.15
–
2.16
) is
unique.

We now extend this solution to
[0,T]
, by applying this argument sequentially.
Let
\{g_{t},q_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
be the above solution over
[0,t_{0}]
.
For any
T>0
and extension of
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
to

\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}+T]}\in\mathcal{L}_{g}(t_{0}+T)
,
Lemma
C.3
shows that
there is a unique solution to the diffusion
(
C.10
) over
[0,t_{0}+T]

with initialization
q_{0}
, which satisfies (
C.14
)
for all
t\in[0,t_{0}+T]
with
K(q_{0})=K
for the above value
K>0
defining
(
C.25
).
The portion of this solution on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
must coincide with the unique
solution to (
C.11
) over
[0,T]

that is initialized at
q_{t_{0}}
and defined by
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]}
.
So for the initialization
q_{t_{0}}
, we must have that
(
C.14
) holds with
K(q_{t_{0}})=K
and the same value

K
as above. Then we may apply the above argument to
[t_{0},2t_{0}]

with
(q_{t_{0}},g_{t_{0}})
in place of
(q_{0},g_{0})
, and then sequentially to

[2t_{0},3t_{0}]
, etc. This proves the existence and uniqueness of the solution

\{(q_{t},g_{t})\}_{t\in[0,T]}
to (
2.15
–
2.16
), as claimed.
The properties
g_{t}(\theta)>0
and
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\sim q_{t}}e^{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\top}{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}}<\infty
follow from the properties of the solutions
guaranteed by Lemmas
C.1
-(1) and

C.3
-(2), and this concludes the proof.
∎

C.4
Proof of Lemma
B.4

Proof of Lemma
B.4
.

Recall the form of
F_{n}
in (
2.11
).
To show
\limsup_{t\to 0}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})\leq F_{n}(q_{0},g_{0})
, recall from
(
C.22
) that for any fixed
T>0
,
q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})
is uniformly
bounded over
t\in[0,T]
. Since
U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})
is also uniformly bounded
over
\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}\leq R
, for any
R>0
,

\lim_{t\to 0}\int\mathbf{1}\{\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{\infty}\leq R\}U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}=\int\mathbf{1}\{\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{\infty}\leq R\}U_{g_{0}}({\bm{\varphi}})q_{0}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}

by the bounded convergence theorem. Applying

U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})<C(\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}^{2}+1)
and Cauchy-Schwarz,

\int\mathbf{1}\{\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{\infty}>R\}|U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})|q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\leq\mathbb{P}_{q_{t}}[\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\|_{\infty}>R]^{1/2}(\mathbb{E}_{q_{t}}[\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\|^{4}]+1)^{1/2}.

By Lemma
C.3
-(2),
\mathbb{E}_{q_{t}}[\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\|^{4}]<C

and
\mathbb{P}_{q_{t}}[|\varphi_{t,j}|>R]\leq e^{-R}\mathbb{E}[e^{|\varphi_{t,j}|}]<Ce^{-R}

for a constant
C>0
, uniformly over
j=1,\ldots,p
and
t\in[0,T]
.
Thus the above display converges to 0 uniformly over
t\in[0,T]

as
R\to\infty
, so

\lim_{t\to 0}\int U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}=\int U_{g_{0}}({\bm{\varphi}})q_{0}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}.

Applying the uniform upper bound
\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\leq C_{0}
and Fatou’s
lemma, we have also

\int[C_{0}-\log q_{0}({\bm{\varphi}})]q_{0}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\leq\liminf_{t\to 0}\int[C_{0}-\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})]q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}},

hence
\limsup_{t\to 0}\int(\log q_{t})q_{t}\leq\int(\log q_{0})q_{0}
.
Combining these statements gives
\limsup_{t\to 0}F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})\leq F_{n}(q_{0},g_{0})
.

To show the form (
B.14
) for the derivative of
F_{n}
,
we will appeal to the following properties of
\{q_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
: For any
fixed
0<t<T
and compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\sup_{{\bm{\varphi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{|\log q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})|}{\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|^{2}+1}

\displaystyle<\infty,

(C.26)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\sup_{{\bm{\varphi}}\in K}\|\nabla q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\|_{2}

\displaystyle<\infty,

(C.27)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\sup_{{\bm{\varphi}}\in K}\|\nabla^{2}q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle<\infty,

(C.28)

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\|\nabla\log q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\|_{2}^{2}q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle<\infty.

(C.29)

The upper bound for
\log q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})
in
(
C.26
) follows from (
C.22
), and the lower bound
follows from
[
BKRS22
, Example 8.2.3]
, where we may take

\beta=1
in light of the form (
C.16
) for the drift

\nabla U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})
.
The next two statements (
C.27
–
C.28
) follow
from the
C^{\infty}
smoothness of
(t,{\bm{\varphi}})\mapsto q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})
.
By Lemma
C.3
-(2), we must have

\mathbb{E}_{q_{s}}[\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{s}\|_{2}^{2}]<C
for some
C>0
uniformly over
s\in[0,T]
. Then (
C.26
) implies
\int|\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})|q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}<\infty
, (
C.16
) implies

\int\|\nabla U_{g_{s}}({\bm{\varphi}})\|_{2}^{2}q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}<C

(C.30)

for some
C>0
uniformly over
s\in[0,T]
, and
the last statement (
C.29
) then follows from

[
BKRS22
, Theorem 7.4.1]
.

Let
{\bm{\eta}}({\bm{\varphi}})=\prod_{j=1}^{p}\eta(\varphi_{j})
where
\eta
is
a smooth, compactly supported bump function on
\mathbb{R}
. Define a truncated version of
F_{n}
by

F_{n}^{\eta}(q_{t},g_{t})=\frac{1}{p}\int{\bm{\eta}}({\bm{\varphi}})[U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})]q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}+\frac{n}{2p}\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{2p}\log\det{\bm{\Sigma}}.

Then for any
t>0
, we may decompose

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}F_{n}^{\eta}(q_{t},g_{t})=\mathrm{I}^{\eta}(t)+\mathrm{II}^{\eta}(t).

Here, we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\int{\bm{\eta}}({\bm{\varphi}})\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})\cdot q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\int\eta(\varphi)\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big({-}\log[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}](\varphi)\Big)\cdot\bar{q}_{t}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\varphi={-}\alpha\int\bigg(\bigg[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\eta\bar{q}_{t}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{t}}\bigg](\theta)-1\bigg)^{2}g_{t}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta,

where these calculations and argument for differentiating under the integral
are the same as in (
B.4
). We also set

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\int{\bm{\eta}}({\bm{\varphi}})[U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})+1]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\int{\bm{\eta}}({\bm{\varphi}})[U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})+1]\cdot\big(\nabla\cdot[q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\nabla U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})]+\Delta q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\big)\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}},

where we may differentiate under the integral in the first line using the
dominated convergence theorem and boundedness of

U_{g_{t}},\nabla U_{g_{t}},\log q_{t},\|\nabla q_{t}\|,|\Delta q_{t}|
in compact
subsets of
(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{p}
as follows from
(
C.26
–
C.28
). Applying integration by parts in

{\bm{\varphi}}
, we then have

\mathrm{II}^{\eta}(t)=\mathrm{III}^{\eta}(t)+\mathrm{IV}^{\eta}(t)
where

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle={-}\frac{1}{p}\int{\bm{\eta}}({\bm{\varphi}})\Big\|\nabla\Big(U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\Big)\Big\|_{2}^{2}q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{IV}^{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle={-}\frac{1}{p}\int[U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})+1]\cdot\nabla{\bm{\eta}}({\bm{\varphi}})^{\top}\nabla[U_{g_{t}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})]q_{t}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}.

Take
\eta\equiv\eta_{R}
such that
\eta_{R}(\varphi)=1
for
|\varphi|\leq R
,

\eta_{R}(\varphi)=0
for
|\varphi|\geq R+1
, and
\eta_{R}^{\prime}(\varphi)
is bounded
by a constant. Then, recalling from the arguments preceding
(
B.2
) that
\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{q}_{t}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g}
is
uniformly bounded, a direct application of the dominated convergence theorem
yields

\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{I}^{\eta_{R}}(s)\mathrm{d}s={-}\alpha\int_{t}^{T}\int_{-M}^{M}\bigg(\bigg[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{q}_{s}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{s}}\bigg](\theta)-1\bigg)^{2}g_{s}(\theta)\mathrm{d}s.

Applying the dominated convergence theorem using
(
C.30
) and (
C.29
), we also have

\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{III}^{\eta_{R}}(s)\mathrm{d}s={-}\frac{1}{p}\int_{t}^{T}\int\Big\|\nabla\Big(U_{g_{s}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\Big)\Big\|_{2}^{2}q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\mathrm{d}s.

Finally, applying again
(
C.30
) and (
C.29
), the bounds

|U_{g_{s}}({\bm{\varphi}})|,|\log q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})|\leq C(\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}^{2}+1)
uniformly
over
s\in[t,T]
, and Holder’s inequality, for some
C,C^{\prime}>0
we have

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}|\mathrm{IV}^{\eta_{R}}(s)|\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\left[\int_{t}^{T}\int\mathbf{1}\{\nabla{\bm{\eta}}_{R}({\bm{\varphi}})\neq 0\}q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{1/4}\cdot\left[\int_{t}^{T}\int(\|{\bm{\varphi}}\|_{2}^{8}+1)q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{1/4}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\cdot\left[\int_{t}^{T}\int\Big\|\nabla\Big(U_{g_{s}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\Big)\Big\|_{2}^{2}q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\mathrm{d}s\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{P}_{q_{s}}[\nabla{\bm{\eta}}_{R}({\bm{\varphi}}_{s})\neq 0]^{1/4}\cdot\sup_{s\in[t,T]}(\mathbb{E}_{q_{s}}[\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{s}\|_{2}^{8}]+1)^{1/4}.

By Lemma
C.3
-(2),
\mathbb{E}_{q_{s}}[\|{\bm{\varphi}}_{s}\|^{8}]<C

and
\mathbb{P}_{q_{s}}[|\varphi_{s,j}|>R]\leq e^{-R}\mathbb{E}[e^{|\varphi_{s,j}|}]<Ce^{-R}

for a constant
C>0
, uniformly over
j=1,\ldots,p
and
s\in[0,T]
. Thus

\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{t}^{T}|\mathrm{IV}^{\eta_{R}}(s)|\mathrm{d}s=0

Combining these statements,

\displaystyle F_{n}(q_{T},g_{T})-F_{n}(q_{t},g_{t})

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}F_{n}^{\eta_{R}}(q_{T},g_{T})-F_{n}^{\eta_{R}}(q_{t},g_{t})

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{t}^{T}\Big(\mathrm{I}^{\eta_{R}}(s)+\mathrm{III}^{\eta_{R}}(s)+\mathrm{IV}^{\eta_{R}}(s)\Big)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{T}\left({-}\alpha\int\bigg(\bigg[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{q}_{s}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g_{s}}\bigg](\theta)-1\bigg)^{2}g_{s}(\theta)\mathrm{d}s{-}\frac{1}{p}\int\Big\|\nabla\Big(U_{g_{s}}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\Big)\Big\|_{2}^{2}q_{s}({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}\right)\mathrm{d}s

where the first equality follows from applying the dominated convergence
theorem to
\lim_{R\to\infty}F_{n}^{\eta_{R}}
. Differentiating both sides in
T

shows that
T\mapsto F_{n}(q_{T},g_{T})
is differentiable at any
T>0
,
with derivative given by (
B.14
).
∎

Appendix D
Derivations of gradient flows

We discuss the derivations of the Wasserstein-2 and Fisher-Rao gradient flow
equations (
2.15
–
2.16
) used in this paper.
This discussion is non-rigorous and only at the
level of formal derivations, and is not used in our proofs.

D.1
General background

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the space of probability density functions on
\mathcal{X}

with finite second moment.
For a smooth curve
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{P}
, we identify its tangent
vector
\dot{\rho}_{t}=\partial_{t}\rho_{t}(\cdot)
at
\rho_{t}

as a function
\dot{\rho}_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. We equip
\mathcal{P}

with the formal structure of a Riemannian manifold, associating to each

\rho\in\mathcal{P}
a tangent space
\tangent_{\rho}\mathcal{P}
of such tangent vectors,
and a metric tensor

g_{\rho}(\cdot,\cdot):\tangent_{\rho}\mathcal{P}\times\tangent_{\rho}\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. This induces a metric, for any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}(\mu,\nu)=\inf\Big\{\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\dot{\rho}_{t}\rVert_{\rho_{t}}:\rho_{0}=\mu,\rho_{1}=\nu\Big\}

(D.1)

where the infimum is over all smooth curves
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{P}

from
\mu
to
\nu
, and

\lVert\zeta\rVert_{\rho}=\sqrt{g_{\rho}(\zeta,\zeta)}
for any
\zeta\in\tangent_{\rho}\mathcal{P}
.
Given a functional
F:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the gradient of
F
at
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
, denoted by
\operatorname{grad}F[\rho]
, is the unique element in
\tangent_{\rho}\mathcal{P}

such that, for all curves
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
with
\rho_{0}=\rho

and
\dot{\rho}_{0}=\zeta\in\tangent_{\rho}\mathcal{P}
,

\displaystyle g_{\rho}\big(\operatorname{grad}F[\rho],\zeta\big)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}F(\rho_{t})=\int\delta F[\rho](x)\zeta(x)\mathrm{d}x

(D.2)

where
\delta F[\rho]:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is the first variation of
F
.
We may then define the gradient flow

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\rho_{t}={-}\operatorname{grad}F[\rho_{t}]
.

For any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}

if the infimum (
D.1
) is achieved by a curve
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
, then
\{\rho_{t}\}
is a geodesic from
\mu
to
\nu
.
We say the functional
F
is geodesically convex at
\rho
if for all geodesics

\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
with
\rho_{0}=\rho
, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}|_{t=0}\mathcal{F}(\rho_{t})\geq 0
.

We now specialize to the Wasserstein-2 and Fisher-Rao geometries, following
closely the presentation in
[
YWR23
]
.

Wasserstein-2 geometry.
For the formal Riemannian structure
underlying the Wasserstein-2 geometry on
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
, the tangent space at

\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
is

\displaystyle\tangent_{\rho}^{W_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{p})=\Big\{\zeta:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}\;\Big|\;\zeta=-\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla u)\text{ for some $u$ satisfying}\int\lVert\nabla u\rVert^{2}\mathrm{d}\rho<\infty\Big\},

equipped with the metric tensor

g^{W_{2}}_{\rho}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\int\left\langle\nabla u_{1},\nabla u_{2}\right\rangle\rho(x)\mathrm{d}x
.
To compute the Wasserstein-2 gradient,
let
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a curve with
\rho_{0}=\rho
and

\dot{\rho}_{0}=\zeta=-\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla v)
. Then (
D.2
)
reads

\displaystyle g_{\rho}^{W_{2}}\big(\operatorname{grad}^{W_{2}}F[\rho],\zeta\big)

\displaystyle=\int\delta F[\rho]\cdot\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x=\int{-}\delta F[\rho]\Big(\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla v)\Big)\mathrm{d}x=\int\left\langle\nabla\delta F[\rho],\nabla v\right\rangle\rho\,\mathrm{d}x,

the last equality applying integration by parts. Comparing with the definition
of
g_{\rho}^{W_{2}}
, this identifies the Wasserstein-2 gradient as

\displaystyle\operatorname{grad}^{W_{2}}F[\rho]=-\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\delta F[\rho]).

(D.3)

Fisher-Rao geometry.
For the formal Riemannian structure
underlying the Fisher-Rao geometry on
\mathcal{P}_{*}(M)
, the tangent space at
\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(M)
is

\displaystyle\tangent_{\rho}^{{\operatorname{FR}}}\mathcal{P}=\Big\{\zeta:[-M,M]\to\mathbb{R}\;\Big|\;\zeta=\rho\Big(\alpha-\int_{-M}^{M}\alpha\mathrm{d}\rho\Big)\text{ for some $\alpha$ satisfying}\int_{-M}^{M}\alpha^{2}\mathrm{d}\rho<\infty\Big\},

equipped with the metric tensor

g^{{\operatorname{FR}}}_{\rho}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\int\frac{\zeta_{1}\cdot\zeta_{2}}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{d}\rho=\int(\alpha_{1}-\int\alpha_{1}\mathrm{d}\rho)(\alpha_{2}-\int\alpha_{2}\mathrm{d}\rho)\mathrm{d}\rho
.
To compute the Fisher-Rao gradient,
let
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a curve with
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
\dot{\rho}_{0}=\zeta=\rho(\alpha-\int\alpha\,\mathrm{d}\rho)
. Then (
D.2
) reads

\displaystyle g^{{\operatorname{FR}}}_{\rho}(\operatorname{grad}^{{\operatorname{FR}}}F[\rho],\zeta)=\int_{-M}^{M}\delta F[\rho]\cdot\zeta\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{-M}^{M}\delta F[\rho]\cdot\Big(\alpha-\int_{-M}^{M}\alpha\mathrm{d}\rho\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{-M}^{M}\Big(\delta F[\rho]-\int_{-M}^{M}\delta F[\rho]\mathrm{d}\rho\Big)\cdot\Big(\alpha-\int_{-M}^{M}\alpha\mathrm{d}\rho\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho.

Comparing with the definition of
g_{\rho}^{{\operatorname{FR}}}
,
this identifies the Fisher-Rao gradient as

\displaystyle\operatorname{grad}^{{\operatorname{FR}}}F[\rho]=\rho\Big(\delta F[\rho]-\int\delta F[\rho]\mathrm{d}\rho\Big).

(D.4)

D.2
Derivatives of
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
and
F_{n}(q,g)

From the form (
1.4
) of
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
, for any positive density

g\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(M)
and bounded
\chi:[-M,M]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\int_{-M}^{M}\chi(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\bar{F}_{n}(g+\varepsilon\chi)

\displaystyle={-}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\int_{[-M,M]^{p}}\exp(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})\frac{\chi(\theta_{j})}{g(\theta_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{p}g(\theta_{i})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\theta}}}{\int_{[-M,M]^{p}}\exp(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})\prod_{i=1}^{p}g(\theta_{i})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\theta}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\int_{-M}^{M}\frac{\chi(\theta_{j})}{g(\theta_{j})}P_{g}({\bm{\theta}}\mid\mathbf{y})=\int_{-M}^{M}{-}\frac{\chi(\theta)}{g(\theta)}\bar{\mu}[g](\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=\int_{-M}^{M}{-}\frac{\bar{\mu}[g](\theta)}{g(\theta)}\chi(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
\bar{\mu}[g]
is the average marginal density of
\mu[g]({\bm{\theta}})=P_{g}({\bm{\theta}}\mid\mathbf{y})

as defined in (
2.7
). Similarly, from the equivalent form
(
2.10
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\bar{F}_{n}(g+\varepsilon\chi)

\displaystyle={-}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\int\frac{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\chi](\varphi_{j})}{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g](\varphi_{j})}P_{g}({\bm{\varphi}}\mid\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\int{-}\frac{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\chi](\varphi)}{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g](\varphi)}\bar{\nu}[g](\varphi)\mathrm{d}\varphi=\int_{-M}^{M}{-}\left[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{\nu}[g]}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g}\right](\theta)\chi(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta,

where the last equality uses the identity

\int f(\varphi)[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\chi](\varphi)\mathrm{d}\varphi=\int_{-M}^{M}[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*f](\theta)\chi(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta
. Thus we identify two
equivalent forms for the first variation of
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
,

\displaystyle\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g](\theta)={-}\frac{\bar{\nu}[g](\theta)}{g(\theta)}+1={-}\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*\frac{\bar{\nu}[g]}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}*g}(\theta)+1,

(D.5)

where the constant
+1
is chosen to center
\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g]
to have mean 0
under
g
. The second form shows (
3.12
), as well as
(
2.3
) when specialized to
\mathbf{X}=\operatorname{Id}
with

\sigma^{2}=\tau^{2}
and
\bar{\nu}[g]=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{y_{i}}
. The first
form shows (
2.6
), in light of the general definition
(
D.4
) for the Fisher-Rao gradient. More generally, for
g\in\mathcal{P}(M)
, the last expression of (
D.5
) still holds and equals to

\displaystyle\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g](\theta)=-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\int_{[-M,M]^{p-1}}\exp(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}(\theta,{\bm{\theta}}_{-j})\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})\prod_{i:i\neq j}g(\mathrm{d}\theta_{i})}{\int_{[-M,M]^{p}}\exp(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})\prod_{i=1}^{p}g(\mathrm{d}\theta_{i})}+1,

which satisfies
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\bar{F}_{n}(g+\varepsilon\chi)=\int_{[-M,M]}\delta\bar{F}_{n}[g](\theta)\chi(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta
for any
\chi
on
[-M,M]
such that
g+\varepsilon\chi
remains a valid distribution for
\varepsilon
in a neighborhood of
0
.

Similarly, for the functional
F_{n}(q,g)
in (
2.11
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}F_{n}(q,g+\varepsilon\chi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\int\frac{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast\chi](\varphi_{j})}{[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast g](\varphi_{j})}q({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}=\int_{-M}^{M}{-}\left[\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast\frac{\bar{q}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast g}\right](\theta)\chi(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}F_{n}(q+\varepsilon\chi,g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\int\big[U_{g}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q({\bm{\varphi}})+1\big]\chi({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}},

where
U_{g}({\bm{\varphi}})
is the negative log-posterior-density
(
3.5
). Then, applying
\int_{-M}^{M}\chi(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta=0

and
\int\chi({\bm{\varphi}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\varphi}}=0
, the first variations of
F_{n}
are

\delta_{g}F_{n}[q,g](\theta)=-\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast\frac{\bar{q}}{\mathcal{N}_{\tau}\ast g}(\theta)+1,\qquad\delta_{q}F_{n}[q,g]({\bm{\varphi}})=\frac{1}{p}\big[U_{g}({\bm{\varphi}})+\log q({\bm{\varphi}})\big],

where, for notational convenience, we have centered this first quantity

\delta_{g}F_{n}[q,g](\theta)
to have mean 0 under
g
.
This leads to the forms (
2.15
–
2.16
) for the gradient flows
(
1.7
), in light of the general definitions
(
D.3
) and (
D.4
) for the Wasserstein-2 and
Fisher-Rao gradients.

D.3
Non-convexity

We provide a concrete example to show that even for the identity design

\mathbf{X}=\operatorname{Id}
, the functional
\bar{F}_{n}(g)
is not geodesically convex under either
the Wasserstein-2 geometry or the Fisher-Rao geometry, even though it is convex
in the sense (
3.4
) in the linear geometry.

For the Wasserstein-2 geometry, consider

g_{0}=g_{\ast}=\delta_{0}
,
g_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1}
,
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)*g_{\ast}
.
The constant-speed Wasserstein-2 geodesic connecting
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
is

g_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{-t}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{t}
for
t\in[0,1]
. Explicit
computation shows

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\bar{F}_{n}(g_{t})=1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{y_{i}^{2}}{\cosh^{2}(ty_{i})},

yielding that
\mathbb{P}_{g_{\ast}}(\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}^{2}t}|_{t=0}\bar{F}_{n}(g_{t})\geq 0)=\mathbb{P}_{g_{\ast}}(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}\leq 1)\approx 1/2
for large
n
.

For the Fisher-Rao geometry, let
g_{*}=\operatorname{Bernoulli}(1/2)
,

g_{0}=\operatorname{Bernoulli}(1/4)
,

g_{1}=\operatorname{Bernoulli}(3/4)
, and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)*g_{\ast}
.
Using the fact
[
MMC23
]
that the geodesic distance in (
D.1
) is

d_{{\operatorname{FR}}}(\text{Bernoulli}(\theta_{1}),\text{Bernoulli}(\theta_{2}))=2|\arcsin(\theta_{1})-\arcsin(\theta_{2})|

for
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in(0,1)
, we have
d_{{\operatorname{FR}}}(g_{0},g_{1})=\pi/3
. The
constant-speed Fisher-Rao geodesic connecting
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
is

g_{t}=\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p_{t})
with
p_{t}=\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi}{6}(t+1))
,
from equating
d_{{\operatorname{FR}}}(g_{0},g_{t})=t\cdot\pi/3
. Explicit computation
shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{g_{\ast}}[\bar{F}_{n}(g_{t})]=-\frac{1}{2}\int\Big(\mathcal{N}(x)+\mathcal{N}(x-1)\Big)\cdot\log\Big[\cos^{2}\Big(\frac{\pi}{6}(t+1)\Big)\mathcal{N}(x)+\sin^{2}\Big(\frac{\pi}{6}(t+1)\Big)\mathcal{N}(x-1)\Big]\mathrm{d}x

is not convex in
t
, where
\mathcal{N}(x)
is the standard
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
density at
x
.

Appendix E
Details of algorithms

CAVI.

We consider the Gibbs variational representation

\bar{F}_{n}(g)=\min_{q}\widetilde{F}_{n}(q,g):=\min_{q}\frac{1}{p}\int_{[-M,M]^{p}}\bigg[\frac{\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}{\bm{\theta}}\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\log g(\theta_{j})+\log q({\bm{\theta}})\bigg]q({\bm{\theta}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\theta}}

in the parametrization by
{\bm{\theta}}
; in other words,
\widetilde{F}_{n}(q,g)
coincides with
{F}_{n}(q,g)
in (
2.11
) for
\tau=0
.
Mean-field variational inference
optimizes
\widetilde{F}_{n}(q,g)
over product distributions
q({\bm{\theta}})=\prod_{j=1}^{p}q_{j}(\theta_{j})
. It is easy to check that, for fixed
g
and

q_{-j}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{j-1},q_{j+1},\ldots,q_{p})
, the minimizer over
q_{j}
has
the explicit form

\displaystyle q_{j}(\theta_{j})\propto\exp\Big({-}\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\theta_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}_{-j}\mathbb{E}_{q_{-j}}[{\bm{\theta}}_{-j}])^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot\theta_{j}\Big)g(\theta_{j})

(E.1)

where
\mathbf{X}_{-j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(p-1)}
is
\mathbf{X}
without the
j
-th column, and

{\bm{\theta}}_{-j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p-1}
is
{\bm{\theta}}
without the
j
-th coordinate. Over
a discrete support
b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}\in[-M,M]
, we represent the prior

g
by probability weights
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{K})
and each
q_{j}

by weights
w_{j}=(w_{j,1},\ldots,w_{j,K})
. Then
w_{j}
is updated as

\displaystyle w_{j,k}=\frac{w_{k}\exp\Big(-\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}b_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}_{-j}\mathbb{E}_{q_{-j}}[{\bm{\theta}}_{-j}])^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot b_{k}\Big)}{\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\exp\Big(-\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}b_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}_{-j}\mathbb{E}_{q_{-j}}[{\bm{\theta}}_{-j}])^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot b_{i}\Big)}\quad\mbox{for all }k=1,\ldots,K,

(E.2)

where
\mathbb{E}_{q_{-j}}[{\bm{\theta}}_{-j}]=(\sum_{k=1}^{K}b_{k}w_{j^{\prime},k})_{j^{\prime}\neq j}
.

Fixing
q=\prod_{j=1}^{p}q_{j}
, we update
g
to minimize

\widetilde{F}_{n}(q,g)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{-M}^{M}g^{\prime\prime}(\theta)^{2}\mathrm{d}\theta=\int_{-M}^{M}[{-}\log g(\theta)]\bar{q}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{-M}^{M}g^{\prime\prime}(\theta)^{2}\mathrm{d}\theta+\text{constant}

(E.3)

where
\bar{q}=p^{-1}\sum_{j}q_{j}
. This is implemented by the weight update

w=\arg\,\min_{w\geq 0,\|w\|_{1}=1}{-}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j,k}}{p}\log w_{k}+\frac{\lambda\Delta}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{Dw}{\Delta}\right]_{k}^{2}

where
D
is the differential quadrature matrix described following
(
2.25
).
When
\lambda=0
, this takes the closed form
w_{k}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j,k}
; when
\lambda>0
, we solve this convex program using CVXR
[
FNB20
]
.
Each iteration of CAVI iteratively updates
(q_{1},\ldots,q_{p},g)
once in this order.

We initialize CAVI from

q_{1}=\ldots=q_{p}=g=\operatorname{Uniform}(\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}\})
.

Gibbs-MCEM.
Under the prior
g(\theta)
, the conditional
posterior density of
\theta_{j}
given
{\bm{\theta}}_{-j}
is

\displaystyle P_{g}(\theta_{j}\mid\mathbf{y},{\bm{\theta}}_{-j})\propto\exp\Big({-}\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\theta_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}_{-j}{\bm{\theta}}_{-j})^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot\theta_{j}\Big)g(\theta_{j}).

(E.4)

Over a discrete support
b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}\in[-M,M]
, this is represented by
the weight vector
w_{j}=(w_{j,1},\ldots,w_{j,K})
with weights

w_{j,k}=\frac{w_{k}\exp\Big(-\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}b_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}_{-j}{\bm{\theta}}_{-j})^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot b_{k}\Big)}{\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\exp\Big(-\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}b_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}_{-j}{\bm{\theta}}_{-j})^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot b_{i}\Big)}\quad\mbox{for all }k=1,\ldots,K

(E.5)

where
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})
are weights for the prior.

Gibbs-MCEM iteratively resamples the coordinates
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{p}
of

{\bm{\theta}}
in sequence
from the conditional law (
E.5
) over the support points

b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}
, fixing the priors weights
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{K})
. Having drawn

T_{\text{iter}}
samples
\theta_{j}^{(1)},\ldots,\theta_{j}^{(T_{\text{iter}})}

of each
j
-th coordinate, it then approximates
\bar{q}
in
(
E.3
) by the empirical distribution of these
samples, yielding the update for prior weights

w=\arg\,\min_{w\geq 0,\|w\|_{1}=1}{-}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\text{iter}}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}1\{\theta_{j}^{(t)}=b_{k}\}}{pT_{\text{iter}}}\log w_{k}+\frac{\lambda\Delta}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{Dw}{\Delta}\right]_{k}^{2}.

We initialize Gibbs-MCEM from
{\bm{\theta}}=0
and

g=\operatorname{Uniform}(\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}\})
, and run the first 200 sampling
iterations without updating prior weights.

Langevin-MCEM.

Langevin-MCEM applies the ULA iterations
(
4.3
) to sample from
P_{g}({\bm{\varphi}}\mid\mathbf{y})
,
fixing the prior weights
w=w_{t}
between every
T_{\text{iter}}
iterations.
After every
T_{\text{iter}}
iterations, denoting by

{\bm{\varphi}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\bm{\varphi}}^{(T_{\text{iter}})}
the sampled iterates,
we sample
S=10000
coordinates
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{S}

uniformly at random from the
pT_{\text{iter}}
total coordinates of

{\bm{\varphi}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\bm{\varphi}}^{(T_{\text{iter}})}
(for reasons of computational
efficiency), and approximate the exact M-step
update (
2.22
) in EM by the prior weight update

w=\arg\,\min_{w\geq 0,\|w\|_{1}=1}{-}\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}\log\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{k}\mathcal{N}_{\tau}(b_{k}-\varphi_{s})\right)+\frac{\lambda\Delta}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{Dw}{\Delta}\right]_{k}^{2}.

In other words, this is a discretized NPMLE with sample size
S
with a smoothness penalty.
This convex program is solved using CVXR.

We initialize Langevin-MCEM from
{\bm{\varphi}}=0
and

q=\operatorname{Uniform}(\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}\})
, and run the first 200 sampling
iterations at large step size
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=1.0
without updating prior
weights, before transitioning to the fixed step size
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}\in\{0.1,1.0\}
with prior weight updates.

EBflow.
EBflow alternately applies the ULA iteration
(
4.3
) and the
w
iteration (
2.26
) with
spline smoothing, or
(
4.5
) and (
2.26
) in the preconditioned
setting. We initialize EBflow also from
{\bm{\varphi}}=0
and

g=\operatorname{Uniform}(\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{K}\})
, and again run the first 200
sampling iterations at large step size
\eta_{t}^{\varphi}=1.0
without updating
prior weights, before transitioning to the log-linear step size decay schedule
(
4.4
).
